\chapter{The model}

\begin{definition}[Block covariance matrix, \S2.1]\label{def:SB}
  \lean{RBM.SB}\leanok
  Let $L\ge 3$ and work on $\ZL$.  Set
  \[ \SB_{ab} \;=\; \tfrac13\,\mathbf 1\!\left(\operatorname{dist}_{\ZL}(a,b)\le 1\right),
     \qquad a,b\in\ZL . \]
  Equivalently $\SB$ is the circulant matrix generated by the kernel supported on
  $\{0,1,-1\}$ with value $1/3$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:L3}
  The paper leaves the hypothesis $L\ge 3$ implicit.  It is needed: for $L=1$ the
  row sums are $1/3$, and for $L=2$ one has $1=-1$ in $\ZL$ so the row sums are
  $2/3$.  See \texttt{docs/paper-deltas.md}.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Elementary properties of $\SB$]\label{lem:SB-basic}
  \lean{RBM.SB_isSymm, RBM.SB_apply_add_right, RBM.sum_SB_row, RBM.SB_mulVec_one}
  \leanok\uses{def:SB}
  $\SB$ is symmetric and translation invariant, each row sums to $1$, and
  $\SB \mathbf 1 = \mathbf 1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{def:SB}
  Symmetry and translation invariance are immediate from the circulant form.  The
  row sums reduce, after the change of variables $b\mapsto a-b$, to the total mass
  of the kernel, which is $3\cdot\frac13=1$ because $0,1,-1$ are distinct in $\ZL$
  when $L\ge 3$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[The band model, \S2.1 and (2.5)]\label{def:model}
  \lean{RBM.SW, RBM.Svar, RBM.Eblk, RBM.Iblk, RBM.Spaper, RBM.Epaper, RBM.splitEquiv}
  \leanok\uses{def:SB}
  With $N=WL$ and blocks $I_a=\{aW,\dots,aW+W-1\}\subseteq\mathbb Z_N$, $a\in\ZL$,
  \[ S_{ij}=\frac1{3W}\sum_a \mathbf 1(i\in I_a)\,\mathbf 1(j\in I_a\cup I_{a+1}\cup I_{a-1}),
     \qquad (E_a)_{ij}=\delta_{ij}W^{-1}\mathbf 1(i\in I_a). \]
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[$S=\SB\otimes S_W$ and properties of $E_a$]\label{lem:model}
  \lean{RBM.Spaper_eq, RBM.Epaper_eq, RBM.split_bijective, RBM.sum_Svar_row,
    RBM.Svar_transpose, RBM.sum_Eblk, RBM.Eblk_conjTranspose, RBM.Eblk_mul_Eblk}
  \leanok\uses{def:model, lem:SB-basic}
  Under $i\mapsto(\lfloor i/W\rfloor, i\bmod W)$, $S=\SB\otimes S_W$ with
  $(S_W)_{\alpha\beta}=W^{-1}$.  $S$ is symmetric with rows summing to $1$;
  $E_a$ is self-adjoint, $E_aE_b=\delta_{ab}W^{-1}E_a$, and $\sum_a E_a=W^{-1}I$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{def:model, lem:SB-basic}
  $i\in I_a$ iff $\lfloor i/W\rfloor=a$, so the sum over $a$ collapses to
  $a=\lfloor i/W\rfloor$; the remaining indicator is $\SB_{\lfloor i/W\rfloor\lfloor j/W\rfloor}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Semicircle law and the $z_t$ flow, Definition 2.7 and Lemma 2.8]\label{lem:2.8}
  \lean{RBM.mE, RBM.mE_mul, RBM.norm_mE, RBM.eq_mE, RBM.msc, RBM.msc_mul, RBM.msc_im_pos,
    RBM.norm_msc_lt_one, RBM.zt, RBM.zt_im, RBM.lemE, RBM.lemT, RBM.mE_lemE, RBM.lemT_eq,
    RBM.lemT_pos, RBM.lemT_lt_one, RBM.abs_lemE_lt_two, RBM.msc_eq_sqrt_mul_mE,
    RBM.eq_inv_sqrt_mul_zt, RBM.msc_add_eq_neg_inv, RBM.abs_lemE_le, RBM.lemT_ge,
    RBM.zt_im_lemma28, RBM.lemma28_quant}
  \leanok
  $m^{(E)}=(-E+i\sqrt{4-E^2})/2$ is the unique root of $m(m+E)=-1$ with $\operatorname{Im}m>0$,
  and $|m^{(E)}|=1$.  For $\operatorname{Im}z>0$, $|m_{sc}(z)|<1$; with
  $E=-2\operatorname{Re}m_{sc}/|m_{sc}|$ and $t=|m_{sc}|^2$ one has
  $m^{(E)}=m_{sc}/|m_{sc}|$, $t=m_{sc}^2/(m^{(E)})^2\in(0,1)$, $|E|<2$,
  $m_{sc}(z)=t^{1/2}m^{(E)}$ (2.38) and $z=t^{-1/2}z^{(E)}_t$ (2.37), where
  $z^{(E)}_t=E+(1-t)m^{(E)}$.  Quantitatively (2.40): if $0<\operatorname{Im}z\le1$ and
  $|\operatorname{Re}z|\le2-\kappa$ then $|E|\le2-\kappa$, $t\ge1/16$ and
  $\tfrac1{16}\operatorname{Im}z\le\operatorname{Im}z_t\le16\operatorname{Im}z$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok
  The roots of $m^2+zm+1$ multiply to $1$, so their imaginary parts have opposite signs,
  and they sum to $-z$.  $u=m_{sc}/|m_{sc}|$ satisfies $u(u+E)=-|u|^2=-1$; uniqueness gives
  $u=m^{(E)}$, and (2.37) follows from $E+u=-1/u$.  For (2.40) write $m_{sc}=re^{i\theta}$:
  $(r+r^{-1})\cos\theta=-\operatorname{Re}z$ gives $|E|=2|\cos\theta|\le|\operatorname{Re}z|$;
  $r^{-1}=|m_{sc}+z|<1+|z|$ gives $t>(1+|z|)^{-2}$; and (2.37) gives
  $\operatorname{Im}z_t=t^{1/2}\operatorname{Im}z$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Deterministic domination, Definition 2.1 (ii)]\label{def:2.1-ii}
  \lean{RBM.UnifDetDom, RBM.DetDom, RBM.detDom_iff}\leanok
  For deterministic $N$-dependent quantities $f,g$ write $f\prec g$ if
  $f\le N^\tau g$ for every $\tau>0$ and all sufficiently large $N$.  The Lean
  definition is uniform in a possibly $N$-dependent parameter $u\in U(N)$, as in
  part (i) of the definition.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Stochastic domination, Definition 2.1 (i)(iii)(iv)]\label{def:2.1-i}
  \lean{RBM.badSet, RBM.StochDom, RBM.NormStochDom, RBM.HighProb, RBM.HighProbIn}\leanok
  \uses{def:2.1-ii}
  For families $\xi,\zeta$ of random variables indexed by $N$ and $u\in U(N)$, $\xi\prec\zeta$
  uniformly in $u$ if for all $\tau,D>0$, eventually
  $\mathbb P(\exists u:\ \xi(N,u)>N^\tau\zeta(N,u))\le N^{-D}$; $A=O_\prec(\zeta)$ means
  $\|A\|\prec\zeta$; $\Xi$ holds w.h.p.\ if $\mathbb P(\Xi^c)\le N^{-D}$ eventually, for every $D$.
  The probability space is fixed and $N$ enters through the random variables.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Properties of stochastic domination]\label{lem:2.1-i-closure}
  \lean{RBM.eventually_two_mul_rpow_le, RBM.rpow_mul_rpow_neg_add, RBM.StochDom.of_subset,
    RBM.StochDom.of_subset_union, RBM.StochDom.of_eventually_empty, RBM.StochDom.of_unifDetDom,
    RBM.StochDom.refl, RBM.StochDom.trans, RBM.StochDom.add, RBM.StochDom.mul,
    RBM.StochDom.const_mul_left, RBM.StochDom.const_mul_right, RBM.StochDom.of_forall_le,
    RBM.StochDom.highProb, RBM.HighProb.mono, RBM.HighProb.inter, RBM.HighProb.biInter,
    RBM.HighProb.highProbIn}
  \leanok\uses{def:2.1-i}
  $\prec$ is reflexive and transitive, closed under sums and products, absorbs constants, and is
  implied by its deterministic version.  Union bound: a bound for each $u$ separately, uniform in
  $u$, gives the uniform statement when $\#U(N)\le N^C$; likewise polynomially many w.h.p.\ events
  hold simultaneously w.h.p.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok
  Every property reduces to an inclusion of failure events and $2N^{-(D+1)}\le N^{-D}$,
  resp.\ $N^C\cdot N^{-(D+C)}=N^{-D}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Closure properties of $\prec$]\label{lem:2.1-ii-closure}
  \lean{RBM.UnifDetDom.refl, RBM.UnifDetDom.trans, RBM.UnifDetDom.add,
    RBM.UnifDetDom.mul, RBM.UnifDetDom.const_mul_left, RBM.UnifDetDom.const_mul_right,
    RBM.UnifDetDom.of_eventually_le_const_mul}
  \leanok\uses{def:2.1-ii}
  On non-negative quantities $\prec$ is reflexive and transitive, closed under sums
  and products, and absorbs positive constant factors on either side; $f\le Cg$
  implies $f\prec g$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{def:2.1-ii}
  Split $N^\tau = N^{\tau/2}N^{\tau/2}$ for transitivity and products; for
  constants use $N^\tau\to\infty$.
\end{proof}

\chapter{The propagator}

\begin{definition}[Propagator, Definition 2.13]\label{def:Theta}
  \lean{RBM.Theta}\leanok\uses{def:SB}
  For $\xi\in\CC$ with $|\xi|<1$ set $\Th_\xi := (1-\xi\SB)^{-1}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Operator norm of $\SB$]\label{lem:norm-SB}
  \lean{RBM.norm_SB}\leanok\uses{lem:SB-basic}
  $\|\SB\|_{\ell^\infty\to\ell^\infty}=1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:SB-basic}
  The $\ell^\infty$ operator norm is the maximum $\ell^1$ row norm; all entries are
  non-negative and every row sums to $1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[$\Th_\xi$ is a two-sided inverse]\label{lem:Theta-inv}
  \lean{RBM.Theta_mul, RBM.mul_Theta, RBM.eq_Theta_of_mul}\leanok
  \uses{def:Theta, lem:norm-SB}
  For $|\xi|<1$ the matrix $1-\xi\SB$ is invertible and $\Th_\xi$ is its two-sided
  inverse.  Moreover any left inverse of $1-\xi\SB$ equals $\Th_\xi$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:norm-SB}
  $\|\xi\SB\|=|\xi|<1$, so the geometric series converges and $1-\xi\SB$ is a unit.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Lemma 2.14 (1): symmetry]\label{lem:Theta-sym}
  \lean{RBM.Theta_transpose}\leanok\uses{lem:Theta-inv, lem:SB-basic}
  $(\Th_\xi)_{xy}=(\Th_\xi)_{yx}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:Theta-inv}
  $\Th^{\mathsf T}(1-\xi\SB) = \Th^{\mathsf T}(1-\xi\SB)^{\mathsf T}
   = \bigl((1-\xi\SB)\Th\bigr)^{\mathsf T} = 1$, so $\Th^{\mathsf T}$ is a left
  inverse; conclude by uniqueness.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Lemma 2.14 (2): translation invariance]\label{lem:Theta-transl}
  \lean{RBM.Theta_apply_add_right}\leanok\uses{lem:Theta-inv, lem:SB-basic}
  $(\Th_\xi)_{xy}=(\Th_\xi)_{x+1,y+1}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:Theta-inv}
  Conjugating by the shift, i.e.\ taking the submatrix along $a\mapsto a+c$,
  fixes $1-\xi\SB$; conclude by uniqueness of the inverse.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Lemma 2.14 (3): commutativity]\label{lem:Theta-comm}
  \lean{RBM.Theta_commute_SB, RBM.Theta_commute}\leanok\uses{lem:Theta-inv}
  $[\SB,\Th_\xi]=[\Th_{\xi'},\Th_\xi]=0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:Theta-inv}
  Inverses of commuting elements commute.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Lemma 2.14 (5): random walk representation]\label{lem:Theta-series}
  \lean{RBM.Theta_eq_tsum}\leanok\uses{lem:Theta-inv}
  $\Th_\xi=\sum_{k\ge 0}\xi^k(\SB)^k$, convergent in operator norm.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:Theta-inv}
  Neumann series.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Row sums]\label{lem:Theta-rowsum}
  \lean{RBM.sum_Theta_row, RBM.Theta_mulVec_one}\leanok
  \uses{lem:Theta-inv, lem:SB-basic}
  $\sum_b (\Th_\xi)_{ab} = (1-\xi)^{-1}$.  Used in (3.47)--(3.48) and in Step 4 of
  the proof of Lemma 3.6.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:SB-basic}
  Apply $\Th_\xi$ to the constant vector and use $\SB\mathbf 1=\mathbf 1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Equation (2.51)]\label{lem:Theta-deriv}
  \lean{RBM.hasDerivAt_Theta_apply, RBM.Theta_sub_Theta}\leanok
  \uses{lem:Theta-inv}
  $\partial_\xi \Th_\xi = \Th_\xi \SB \Th_\xi$, entrywise.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:Theta-inv}
  The resolvent identity
  $\Th_\zeta-\Th_\xi=(\zeta-\xi)\,\Th_\zeta\SB\Th_\xi$
  together with continuity of $\xi\mapsto\Th_\xi$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform $\ell^1$ and $\ell^\infty$ bound]\label{lem:Theta-bounds}
  \lean{RBM.norm_Theta_le, RBM.sum_norm_Theta_row_le, RBM.norm_Theta_apply_le,
    RBM.sum_norm_Theta_row_of_real}\leanok
  \uses{lem:Theta-series, lem:norm-SB}
  $\max_a\sum_b |(\Th_\xi)_{ab}| \le (1-|\xi|)^{-1}$, and in particular
  $|(\Th_\xi)_{ab}| \le (1-|\xi|)^{-1}$.  For real $\xi = t$ the entries are positive and
  the $\ell^1$ norm is \emph{exactly} $(1-t)^{-1}$.
  \emph{This is not yet (3.35)--(3.36)}: see Lemma~\ref{lem:3.35}.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:Theta-series}
  Sum the Neumann series in operator norm.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Band structure and a first decay bound]\label{lem:Theta-band}
  \lean{RBM.SB_pow_apply_eq_zero, RBM.Theta_eq_geom_add, RBM.norm_Theta_apply_le_pow}\leanok
  \uses{lem:Theta-series, lem:Theta-bounds}
  $(\SB)^k$ vanishes outside the band of width $k$, and consequently
  \[ \bigl|(\Th_\xi)_{xy}\bigr| \;\le\;
     \frac{|\xi|^{\operatorname{dist}(x,y)}}{1-|\xi|} . \]
  This is exponential decay with length $1/\log(1/|\xi|)$, weaker than (2.52).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:Theta-series}
  Truncate the Neumann series at order $d=\operatorname{dist}(x,y)$:
  $\Th_\xi=\sum_{k<d}(\xi\SB)^k+(\xi\SB)^d\Th_\xi$, and the finite sum has
  vanishing $(x,y)$ entry.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Fourier representation, Appendix B (B.1)]\label{eq:B.1}
  \lean{RBM.Shat, RBM.Shat_eq_cos, RBM.SB_mulVec_char, RBM.one_sub_mul_Shat_ne_zero,
    RBM.fourierKernel, RBM.Theta_eq_circulant_fourierKernel, RBM.Theta_apply_fourier}
  \leanok\uses{def:Theta, lem:Theta-inv}
  The plane wave $e_p(x)=e^{ipx}$, $p\in\frac{2\pi}{L}\ZL$, is an eigenvector of
  $\SB$ with eigenvalue $\widehat S(p)=(1+2\cos p)/3$.  Since $|\widehat S(p)|\le 1$,
  $1-\xi\widehat S(p)\neq 0$ for $|\xi|<1$, and
  \[ (\Th_\xi)_{xy} \;=\; \frac1L\sum_{p\in\frac{2\pi}{L}\ZL}
     \frac{e^{ip(x-y)}}{1-\xi\widehat S(p)} . \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{def:Theta, lem:Theta-inv}
  Call the right-hand side $K(x-y)$.  By the eigenvector relation and orthogonality
  of characters, $K-\xi\,\SB K=\delta_0$; the circulant matrix generated by $K$ is
  therefore a left inverse of $1-\xi\SB$, and uniqueness of the inverse closes it.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Two-sided bound on the symbol, Appendix B (B.3)]\label{eq:B.3}
  \lean{RBM.theta, RBM.abs_theta_le_pi, RBM.Shat_eq_cos_theta, RBM.le_one_sub_cos,
    RBM.norm_one_sub_mul_Shat_le, RBM.le_norm_one_sub_mul_Shat,
    RBM.norm_one_sub_mul_Shat_asymp}\leanok
  \uses{eq:B.1}
  With $\theta(p)=2\pi\,\tilde p/L\in[-\pi,\pi]$, $\tilde p$ the representative of $p$ closest
  to $0$: uniformly in $L$, $|\xi|<1$ and $p$,
  \[ \frac{|1-\xi|+\theta(p)^2}{6\pi^2} \;\le\; |1-\xi\widehat S(p)| \;\le\;
     |1-\xi|+\theta(p)^2 . \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{eq:B.1}
  $s=1-\widehat S=\frac23(1-\cos\theta)$ and $\frac{2}{\pi^2}\theta^2\le1-\cos\theta\le
  \frac12\theta^2$ (Jordan at $\theta/2$).  Upper bound: $1-\xi\widehat S=(1-\xi)+\xi s$.
  Lower bound: if $\widehat S\ge\frac12$, $1-\xi\widehat S=s+\widehat S(1-\xi)$ with
  $\operatorname{Re}(1-\xi)>0$ gives $|1-\xi\widehat S|\ge s$ and
  $\ge\widehat S|1-\xi|-s$; if $\widehat S<\frac12$, $|1-\xi\widehat S|>\frac12$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Infinite-volume kernel, (B.2)]\label{eq:B.2}
  \lean{RBM.Dxi, RBM.kernelFun, RBM.Kinf}\leanok
  $K_{\xi,\infty}(u)=\frac1{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\frac{e^{ipu}}{1-\xi\widehat S(p)}\,dp$,
  $u\in\mathbb Z$, with $D_\xi(z)=1-\xi(1+2\cos z)/3$ for complex $z$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Shifted symbol, (B.4)]\label{eq:B.4}
  \lean{RBM.norm_cos_add_mul_I_sub_le, RBM.cZero, RBM.le_norm_Dxi, RBM.Dxi_ne_zero}\leanok
  \uses{eq:B.3, eq:B.2}
  With $\kappa=|1-\xi|^{1/2}$ and $c_0=1/(16\pi^2)$: for $|p|\le\pi$ and $|\eta|\le c_0\kappa$,
  $|D_\xi(p+i\eta)|\ge(\kappa^2+p^2)/(12\pi^2)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{eq:B.3, eq:B.2}
  $|\cos(p+i\eta)-\cos p|\le(\cosh\eta-1)+|\sin p||\sinh\eta|\le\eta^2+2|p||\eta|
  \le2c_0(\kappa^2+p^2)$, absorbed by the lower bound $(\kappa^2+p^2)/(6\pi^2)$ of (B.3).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Contour shift and decay of $K_{\xi,\infty}$, (B.5)]\label{eq:B.5}
  \lean{RBM.kernelFun_add_two_pi, RBM.integral_kernelFun_shift, RBM.norm_kernelFun_shift_le,
    RBM.integral_inv_sq_add_sq_le, RBM.norm_Kinf_le, RBM.norm_Kinf_le_exists}\leanok
  \uses{eq:B.4}
  $|K_{\xi,\infty}(u)|\le\frac{6\pi^2}{\kappa}\,e^{-c_0\kappa|u|}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{eq:B.4}
  The integrand is $2\pi$-periodic and holomorphic on $[-\pi,\pi]\times[0,\eta]$ by (B.4),
  so Cauchy's theorem on the rectangle (vertical sides cancel) moves the contour to
  $\mathbb R+i\eta$, $\eta=c_0\kappa\operatorname{sgn}u$.  There $|e^{i(p+i\eta)u}|=e^{-c_0\kappa|u|}$
  and $\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}dp/(\kappa^2+p^2)\le\pi/\kappa$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Periodization, after (B.2)]\label{eq:B.2-per}
  \lean{RBM.integral_exp_int, RBM.Kinf_sub_SB, RBM.summable_Kinf_shift, RBM.perSum, RBM.perK,
    RBM.perK_sub_SB_mulVec, RBM.Theta_eq_circulant_perK, RBM.Theta_apply_periodize}\leanok
  \uses{eq:B.5, def:Theta, lem:Theta-inv}
  $(\Th_\xi)_{xy}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb Z}K_{\xi,\infty}(u+nL)$, $u=x-y$, the sum converging
  absolutely.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{eq:B.5, def:Theta, lem:Theta-inv}
  Instead of Fourier inversion: $\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{ipw}dp=2\pi\delta_{w,0}$ gives
  $K_\infty-\xi\SB K_\infty=\delta_0$ on $\mathbb Z$; by (B.5) the periodization converges
  absolutely and solves the same equation on $\ZL$, so its circulant inverts $1-\xi\SB$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[(3.35)--(3.36): short and long edges]\label{lem:3.35}
  \lean{RBM.norm_Theta_short_edge_le, RBM.sum_norm_Theta_row_le_complex,
    RBM.sum_norm_Theta_short_edge_le, RBM.norm_Theta_long_edge_le,
    RBM.sum_norm_Theta_long_edge_le}
  \leanok\uses{lem:Theta-bounds, lem:Theta-decay, lem:2.8, lem:edges, lem:Theta-decay-complex}
  The paper states, with $\ell_t$ the decay length and $\eta_t = \operatorname{Im} z_t$,
  \[ \|\Th_{tm^2}\|_{\max} \prec 1, \quad \|\Th_{t|m|^2}\|_{\max} \prec \frac1{\ell_t\eta_t};
     \qquad
     \|\Th_{tm^2}\|_{1} \prec 1, \quad \|\Th_{t|m|^2}\|_{1} \prec \frac1{\eta_t} . \]
  \emph{All four are now proved}; the analysis below records what each one needed, since a
  first reading suggested (wrongly) that the uniform bound already covered them.
  \begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Long edge} ($\xi = t|m|^2 = t$, real).  The $\max$ bound follows from
      (2.52) (Lemma~\ref{lem:Theta-decay}) and the $\ell^1$ bound is the identity
      $(1-t)^{-1}$ of Lemma~\ref{lem:Theta-bounds} --- \emph{provided} the dictionary
      $1-t \asymp \eta_t$, which is part of the quantitative half (2.40) of Lemma 2.8
      that is currently deferred.
    \item \textbf{Short edge} ($\xi = tm^2$ with $m$ non-real, $|m| = 1$).  Here
      $|\xi| = t \to 1$, so the uniform bound $(1-|\xi|)^{-1}$ of
      Lemma~\ref{lem:Theta-bounds} \textbf{blows up} and does not give $\prec 1$.  What is
      needed is a lower bound on $|1-tm^2|$, which is where the complex-$\xi$ estimate
      (Lemma~\ref{lem:Theta-decay-complex}) enters.
  \end{itemize}
  The two analytic ingredients are now in place (Lemma~\ref{lem:edges}); what remains is
  to assemble them.
\end{lemma}

\begin{lemma}[Short versus long edge: the exact modulus]\label{lem:edges}
  \lean{RBM.normSq_one_sub_mul_sq_mE, RBM.two_mul_mul_le_normSq_one_sub,
    RBM.zt_im_le, RBM.le_zt_im, RBM.norm_short_edge, RBM.le_normSq_one_sub_short,
    RBM.sqrt_le_norm_one_sub_short, RBM.half_le_ellHat, RBM.norm_Theta_short_edge_le}
  \leanok\uses{lem:2.8}
  For $m = m^{(E)}$ with $|E|\le 2$,
  \[ \bigl|1 - t m^2\bigr|^2 \;=\; (1-t)^2 + t\,(4 - E^2) , \]
  an identity, not an estimate.  The first term is what the long edge $\xi = t$ gives on
  its own; the second is the gap that makes the short edge non-degenerate, and it is
  controlled exactly by the paper's hypothesis $|E| \le 2-\kappa$, which yields
  $|1 - tm^2|^2 \ge 2t\kappa$.  At $E = \pm 2$ the gap closes and the short edge
  degenerates into the long one.

  The same computation supplies the dictionary between the paper's $\eta_t$ and the
  parameter $1-t$ our estimates use: by Definition 2.7,
  $\eta_t = (1-t)\operatorname{Im}m^{(E)}$ with
  $\operatorname{Im}m^{(E)} = \sqrt{4-E^2}/2 \in (0,1]$, so
  $(1-t)\sqrt{2\kappa}/2 \le \eta_t \le 1-t$.  This needs only Definition 2.7 --- not the
  deferred quantitative half (2.40) of Lemma 2.8, as first supposed.

  Feeding this into the complex-$\xi$ estimate (Lemma~\ref{lem:Theta-decay-complex}) gives
  the first half of (3.35): $\bigl|(\Th_{tm^2})_{xy}\bigr| \le 2C/\sqrt\kappa$, uniformly in
  $t\in[0,1)$, in $L$ and in $x,y$.  Note the bound holds for \emph{all} $t\in[0,1]$, not
  only $t$ bounded away from $0$: the minimum of $(1-t)^2+2t\kappa$ is $2\kappa-\kappa^2\ge\kappa$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{lemma}[The characteristic root $\rho(\xi)$]\label{lem:rho}
  \lean{RBM.rho, RBM.norm_root_ne_one, RBM.norm_rho_lt_one}\leanok\uses{def:Theta}
  Write $c = 3/\xi - 1$.  The characteristic equation of the three-term recursion
  behind $(1-\xi\SB)\Th_\xi = 1$ is $\rho^2 - c\rho + 1 = 0$, whose two roots
  multiply to $1$.  For $0 < |\xi| < 1$ neither root lies on the unit circle, so
  exactly one has modulus $< 1$; call it $\rho(\xi)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{def:Theta}
  If $|\rho| = 1$ then $|c| = |\rho + \rho^{-1}| \le |\rho| + |\rho^{-1}| = 2$,
  hence $3/|\xi| = |c+1| \le 3$, i.e.\ $|\xi| \ge 1$.  This is the same statement
  as $1 - \xi\widehat S(p) \ne 0$ in the Fourier picture of Appendix B, and it is
  exactly what fails for $|\xi| \ge 1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Closed form of the propagator]\label{lem:closed-form}
  \lean{RBM.theta_eq_circulant, RBM.theta_apply_closed_form}\leanok
  \uses{lem:rho, lem:Theta-inv}
  For $0 < |\xi| < 1$,
  \[ (\Th_\xi)_{xy} = A(\xi)\bigl(\rho^{\,d} + \rho^{\,L-d}\bigr),\qquad
     d = \overline{(x-y)} \in [0,L),\qquad
     A(\xi) = \frac{3\rho}{\xi\,(\rho^L-1)(\rho^2-1)} . \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:rho, lem:Theta-inv}
  The kernel satisfies $k(u-1) + k(u+1) - c\,k(u) = -(3/\xi)\delta_{u,0}$.  The
  symmetric shape makes the value at $n=L$ agree with the value at $n=0$, so the
  wrap-around at $d = L-1$ falls under the generic case and $d = 0$ is the only
  special one; the defect there is what fixes $A(\xi)$.  Conclude by uniqueness
  of the inverse.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Decay rate for real spectral parameter]\label{lem:rho-rate}
  \lean{RBM.rho_real_bounds}\leanok\uses{lem:rho}
  For $0 < t < 1$, $\rho(t)$ is real, lies in $(0,1)$, and
  \[ \sqrt{1-t} \;\le\; 1 - \rho(t) \;\le\; \sqrt3\,\sqrt{1-t} . \]
  So the decay length $1/(1-\rho)$ is of order $(1-t)^{-1/2}$.  This is the
  long-edge case $\xi = t|m|^2$, where $|m| = 1$ makes $\xi$ real; it is the case
  that needs the sharp length.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:rho}
  The discriminant $(3/t-1)^2 - 4$ is a positive real, so both roots are real and
  $\rho \in (0,1)$; then $1 - \|\rho\| = 1 - \rho$, which is the direction the
  decay estimate needs.  Combine with the exact identity
  $(1-\rho)^2 = (1-\xi)(1+\rho+\rho^2)$ and $1 \le 1+\rho+\rho^2 \le 3$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Decay rate for complex spectral parameter]\label{lem:rho-rate-complex}
  \lean{RBM.sub_re_le_of_poly, RBM.three_mul_norm_rho_lt, RBM.sq_one_sub_norm_rho_le,
    RBM.norm_one_sub_xi_le, RBM.rho_complex_bounds}\leanok\uses{lem:rho}
  For all $0<|\xi|<1$,
  \[ \tfrac18\,|1-\xi| \;\le\; (1-|\rho(\xi)|)^2 \;\le\; 3\,|1-\xi| , \]
  so the decay length is $|1-\xi|^{-1/2}$ for every admissible $\xi$, including the
  short-edge case $\xi=tm^2$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:rho}
  Upper bound: $1-|\rho|\le|1-\rho|$ and $|1-\rho|^2=|1-\xi|\,|1+\rho+\rho^2|\le3|1-\xi|$.
  Lower bound: $|\xi|<1$ is $3|\rho|<|1+\rho+\rho^2|$.  With $p=\operatorname{Re}\rho$,
  $r=|\rho|$ this reads $g>0$, where exactly
  $g=-4(r-p-(1-r)^2)(r+p)+(r^2-4r+1)(r^2+1-2p)$.  For $r\ge\frac12$ the second term is
  $\le0$, so $r-p\le(1-r)^2$, whence $|1-\rho|^2=(1-r)^2+2(r-p)\le3(1-r)^2$ and
  $|1-\xi|=|1-\rho|^2/|1+\rho+\rho^2|\le2(1-r)^2$.  For $r<\frac12$, $|1-\xi|<2<8(1-r)^2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The prefactor of (2.52)]\label{lem:prefactor}
  \lean{RBM.AA_eq, RBM.pow_mul_one_add_le, RBM.le_one_sub_pow, RBM.ellHat,
    RBM.ellHat_mul_one_sub_le, RBM.norm_AA_le_of_real}
  \leanok\uses{lem:closed-form, lem:rho-rate}
  The constant of the closed form simplifies to
  \[ A(\xi) \;=\; \frac{1-\rho}{(1-\xi)\,(1-\rho^L)\,(1+\rho)} , \]
  and for real $\xi = t \in (0,1)$,
  \[ |A(t)| \;\le\; \frac{4}{(1-t)\,\hat\ell(t)},
     \qquad \hat\ell(t) = \min\bigl((1-t)^{-1/2}, L\bigr). \]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:closed-form, lem:rho-rate}
  Substituting $(1-\xi)\cdot 3\rho = \xi(1-\rho)^2$ into
  $A = 3\rho/[\xi(\rho^L-1)(\rho^2-1)]$ cancels one factor $1-\rho$ against
  $\rho^2-1$ and converts $1/\xi$ into $1/(1-\xi)$.  For the bound, Bernoulli
  $1+Lx \le (1+x)^L$ together with $(1-x)(1+x)\le 1$ gives
  $1-\rho^L \ge L(1-\rho)/(1+L(1-\rho))$, which is $\asymp \min(1, L(1-\rho))$;
  the two regimes of the $\min$ in $\hat\ell$ are exactly $L(1-\rho)\gtrsim 1$ and
  $L(1-\rho)\lesssim 1$, and $\sqrt{1-t}\le 1-\rho\le\sqrt 3\sqrt{1-t}$ converts
  between them.  No exponentials are needed.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[(2.52) for complex $\xi$, Fourier route]\label{lem:Theta-decay-complex}
  \lean{RBM.tsum_exp_neg_abs_shift_le, RBM.norm_Theta_apply_le_large,
    RBM.norm_Theta_apply_le_small, RBM.norm_Theta_apply_le_complex,
    RBM.norm_Theta_apply_le_exists}\leanok
  \uses{eq:B.2-per, eq:B.5, eq:B.3, eq:B.1}
  For every $|\xi|<1$, with $\hat\ell(\xi)=\min(|1-\xi|^{-1/2},L)$ and $c_0=1/(16\pi^2)$,
  \[ \bigl|(\Th_\xi)_{xy}\bigr|\le\frac{C\,e^{-c_0\|x-y\|/\hat\ell(\xi)}}{|1-\xi|\,\hat\ell(\xi)} ,\]
  $C$ independent of $L$ and $\xi$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{eq:B.2-per, eq:B.5, eq:B.3, eq:B.1}
  $\kappa L\ge1$: periodization and (B.5); the terms $n\ge0$ and $n<0$ are geometric series
  from $e^{-c_0\kappa\|u\|}$ with ratio $\le e^{-c_0}$.  $\kappa L<1$: (B.1) and (B.3); the zero
  mode gives $1/(\kappa^2L)$, the rest $\frac32 ZL$ with $Z=\sum_{n\neq0}n^{-2}$, and
  $L\le1/(\kappa^2L)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Lemma 2.14 (4): exponential decay, (2.52)]\label{lem:Theta-decay}
  \lean{RBM.zdist_le, RBM.norm_Theta_apply_le_of_real}
  \leanok\uses{lem:Theta-band, lem:closed-form, lem:rho-rate, lem:prefactor}
  With $\hat\ell(\xi):=\min(|1-\xi|^{-1/2},L)$ and real $\xi = t \in (0,1)$,
  \[ \bigl|(\Th_t)_{xy}\bigr| \;\le\;
     \frac{8e\,\cdot\,e^{-\|x-y\|/\hat\ell(t)}}{(1-t)\cdot\hat\ell(t)} . \]
  The paper's $C$ and $c$ are here the explicit $8e$ and $1$.  Real $\xi$ is the
  long-edge case $\xi = t|m|^2 = t$ of the paper (recall $|m| = 1$), which is the
  case that needs the sharp length scale.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:Theta-band, lem:closed-form, lem:rho-rate, lem:prefactor}
  Two regimes.  If $(1-t)^{-1/2}\le L$ the exponential does the work:
  $\rho \le e^{-(1-\rho)} \le e^{-\sqrt{1-t}} = e^{-1/\hat\ell}$.  If $L \le (1-t)^{-1/2}$
  the exponential is useless ($e^{-d/L}\ge e^{-1}$ for $d \le L$) and the prefactor
  bound $|A| \le 4/((1-t)L)$ of Lemma~\ref{lem:prefactor} carries the estimate alone.
  No contour shift and no Poisson summation.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[$\ell^1$ bound on a row, after (2.52)]\label{lem:Theta-l1}
  \lean{RBM.exists_pos_Theta_apply_of_real, RBM.sum_norm_Theta_row_of_real}
  \leanok\uses{lem:closed-form, lem:Theta-rowsum}
  For real $\xi = t \in (0,1)$ every entry of $\Th_t$ is a positive real, hence
  \[ \sum_b \bigl|(\Th_t)_{ab}\bigr| \;=\; \sum_b (\Th_t)_{ab} \;=\; (1-t)^{-1} . \]
  The paper states $\le C(1-t)^{-1}$; positivity turns the inequality into an identity
  with constant $1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:closed-form, lem:Theta-rowsum}
  In the closed form $A(\rho^d+\rho^{L-d})$ the root $\rho$ lies in $(0,1)$ and all four
  factors of $A = (1-\rho)/[(1-t)(1-\rho^L)(1+\rho)]$ are positive, so the $\ell^1$ norm of
  a row equals its signed sum, which is $(1-\xi)^{-1}$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:Theta-rowsum}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Difference estimates (2.53)--(2.54)]\label{lem:Theta-diff}
  \lean{RBM.kern_succ_sub, RBM.kern_second_diff, RBM.norm_AA_mul_one_sub_rho_le,
    RBM.norm_AA_mul_one_sub_rho_sq_le, RBM.norm_Theta_sub_shift_le,
    RBM.kern_wrap_second, RBM.norm_Theta_second_diff_le, RBM.norm_Theta_second_diff_diag_le,
    RBM.norm_Theta_second_diff_le_pow, RBM.mul_exp_neg_le_exp_neg_one,
    RBM.norm_Theta_second_diff_le_inv_dist}
  \leanok\uses{lem:Theta-decay, lem:prefactor}
  For real $\xi = t \in (0,1)$,
  \[ \bigl|(\Th_t)_{x,y}-(\Th_t)_{x,y+1}\bigr| \le \frac{8\sqrt3}{\hat\ell(t)\,(1-t)^{1/2}},
     \qquad
     \bigl|2(\Th_t)_{x,y}-(\Th_t)_{x,y+1}-(\Th_t)_{x,y-1}\bigr| \le \frac{24}{\hat\ell(t)}
     \quad (x \ne y). \]
  Both are explicit constants, hence stronger than the paper's $\prec$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:Theta-decay, lem:prefactor}
  Writing $L = n+1+j$, the kernel differences telescope:
  $\mathrm{kern}(n{+}1)-\mathrm{kern}(n) = A(1-\rho)(\rho^j-\rho^n)$ and
  $\mathrm{kern}(n{+}1)+\mathrm{kern}(n{-}1)-2\,\mathrm{kern}(n) = A(1-\rho)^2(\rho^{n-1}+\rho^{j})$.
  Each lattice difference costs exactly one factor $1-\rho \asymp (1-t)^{1/2}$, i.e. one
  inverse decay length.  \emph{The diagonal $x = y$ is genuinely different}: there the
  kernel $\rho^d+\rho^{L-d}$ has a cusp and only one factor $1-\rho$ comes out, giving
  $4\|A(1-\rho)\| = O(1)$ --- consistent with the paper's right-hand side
  $1/(\|x-y\|+1)$, which is $1$ at $x = y$.

  \emph{A gap found by re-reading the paper's statement}: the bound $24/\hat\ell$ carries no
  decay, and therefore does \emph{not} imply the paper's $1/(\|x-y\|+1)$ once
  $\|x-y\|\gg\hat\ell$.  Keeping the factor $\rho^{\|x-y\|-1}$ closes it:
  $\bigl|2\Th_{x,y}-\Th_{x,y+1}-\Th_{x,y-1}\bigr| \le 60/(\|x-y\|+1)$, proved in the regime
  $\hat\ell = L$ from $\|x-y\|\le L/2$ and in the regime $\hat\ell = (1-t)^{-1/2}$ from
  $u e^{-u}\le e^{-1}\le 1/2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Removing the zero mode, end of \S7.2]\label{lem:zero-mode}
  \lean{RBM.onesMat, RBM.SBTilde, RBM.ThetaTilde, RBM.zeroMode, RBM.onesMat_apply,
    RBM.onesMat_mul_onesMat, RBM.SB_mul_onesMat, RBM.onesMat_mul_SB, RBM.Theta_mul_onesMat,
    RBM.onesMat_mul_Theta, RBM.one_sub_smul_SBTilde, RBM.ThetaTilde_eq, RBM.ThetaTilde_apply,
    RBM.ThetaTilde_sub_ThetaTilde, RBM.norm_mul_one_sub_lt_one, RBM.norm_one_sub_mul_le,
    RBM.re_le_one_of_norm_le_one, RBM.normSq_one_sub_mul, RBM.le_norm_one_sub_mul,
    RBM.norm_one_sub_mul_ge_zeta, RBM.norm_one_sub_mul_comparable, RBM.sqrt_le_two_mul_sqrt,
    RBM.ellHat_le_two_mul, RBM.zeroMode_setup, RBM.norm_ThetaTilde_sub_zeroMode_le,
    RBM.norm_ThetaTilde_sub_shift_le, RBM.norm_ThetaTilde_second_diff_le}
  \leanok\uses{lem:Theta-rowsum, lem:Theta-decay-complex, lem:Theta-diff-complex}
  For $\tilde S^{(B)}=(1-\zeta)S^{(B)}+(\zeta/L)J$: $(1-\xi\tilde S)^{-1}=\Theta_{\xi(1-\zeta)}+\alpha J$ with an
  explicit scalar $\alpha$, so differences of entries agree with those of $\Theta_{\xi(1-\zeta)}$;
  $|1-\xi|/2\le|1-\xi(1-\zeta)|\le2|1-\xi|$ when $\zeta\le\min(1/2,|1-\xi|)$; hence
  (2.52)--(2.54) hold for $\tilde S$ up to the constant zero mode.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok
  $J$ acts only on constants and $\Theta_\xi\mathbf 1=(1-\xi)^{-1}\mathbf 1$: Sherman--Morrison by hand.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform differences of the long-edge propagator]\label{lem:long-diff}
  \lean{RBM.AA_mul_one_sub_rho_eq, RBM.abs_pow_sub_pow_le, RBM.Theta_sub_shift_eq_kern,
    RBM.Theta_sub_shift_eq, RBM.AA_mul_one_sub_rho_real, RBM.norm_Theta_sub_shift_le_uniform,
    RBM.gradK, RBM.Theta_sub_shift_real, RBM.sum_row_val_eq, RBM.sum_norm_Theta_sub_shift_le,
    RBM.Theta_second_diff_eq, RBM.norm_Theta_second_diff_le_three,
    RBM.sum_norm_Theta_second_diff_le}
  \leanok\uses{lem:Theta-diff}
  For real $\xi = t\in(0,1)$ and every $L\ge3$:
  $|(\Th_t)_{x,y}-(\Th_t)_{x,y+1}|\le 3/2$,
  $\sum_y|(\Th_t)_{x,y}-(\Th_t)_{x,y+1}|\le 3\hat\ell(t)$,
  $|2(\Th_t)_{x,y}-(\Th_t)_{x,y\pm1}|\le 3$ and
  $\sum_y|2(\Th_t)_{x,y}-(\Th_t)_{x,y+1}-(\Th_t)_{x,y-1}|\le 6$.
  (Not stated in the paper; used for Lemma 3.11 in place of (3.66).)
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:Theta-diff}
  $A(1-\rho) = (1+\rho+\rho^2)/((1+\rho)(1-\rho^L))$ exactly, and $|\rho^j-\rho^n|\le1-\rho^L$
  for $j,n\le L$.  The gradient bound $C/(\hat\ell\sqrt{1-t})$ of (2.53) is sharp only when
  $\hat\ell = (1-t)^{-1/2}$; for $\hat\ell = L$ the gradient is $O(1)$.  The row sums are
  geometric sums of $|\rho^{L-1-n}-\rho^n|$.
\end{proof}

\chapter{The primitive equation and $\Kcal$}

\begin{lemma}[(2.53) for complex $\xi$]\label{lem:Theta-diff-complex}
  \lean{RBM.one_add_rho_sq_mul, RBM.half_le_norm_one_add_rho,
    RBM.norm_one_sub_rho_sq_le_three, RBM.norm_AA_mul_one_sub_rho_le_complex,
    RBM.ellHat_mul_sqrt_le, RBM.norm_Theta_sub_shift_le_complex,
    RBM.norm_one_sub_rho_le_sqrt, RBM.norm_AA_mul_one_sub_rho_sq_le_complex,
    RBM.norm_Theta_second_diff_le_complex, RBM.norm_Theta_second_diff_le_inv_dist_complex}
  \leanok\uses{lem:Theta-diff, lem:closed-form, lem:rho}
  \[ \bigl|(\Th_\xi)_{x,y}-(\Th_\xi)_{x,y+1}\bigr| \;\le\;
     \frac{144}{\hat\ell(\xi)\,\|1-\xi\|^{1/2}} \qquad (\|\xi\| < 1). \]
  The identities behind the real case hold for every $\xi$; only the bounds on $A(1-\rho)$
  had to be redone.  Two new ingredients: the exact
  $\|1-\rho\|^2 = \|1-\xi\|\,\|1+\rho+\rho^2\|$ with $\|1+\rho+\rho^2\|\le 3$, and
  $\|1+\rho\| \ge 1/2$ --- the root never approaches $-1$ on the disc, since
  $(1+\rho)^2\xi = \rho(3+\xi)$ would force $\xi$ towards $-3$.

  The second difference follows the same route and lands on the paper's form,
  \[ \bigl|2(\Th_\xi)_{x,y}-(\Th_\xi)_{x,y+1}-(\Th_\xi)_{x,y-1}\bigr|
     \;\le\; \frac{1728}{\|x-y\|+1} , \]
  keeping the factor $\|\rho\|^{\|x-y\|-1}$ throughout, as the real case taught us to.
  \textbf{With this, all six parts of Lemma 2.14 hold on the whole disc $\|\xi\|<1$},
  and the dyadic decomposition of Appendix B is not needed for them.
\end{lemma}

\begin{definition}[Loops and cut-and-glue operators, Definitions 2.9--2.10]\label{def:loops}
  \lean{RBM.LoopIdx, RBM.LoopIdx.cutGlue, RBM.LoopIdx.cutGlueL, RBM.LoopIdx.cutGlueR}
  \leanok
  The operators $\Gcal^{(a)}_k$, $\Gcal^{(a),L}_{k,l}$, $\Gcal^{(a),R}_{k,l}$ act on the
  index data $(\boldsymbol\sigma,\mathbf a)$ of $\langle\prod_i G(\sigma_i)E_{a_i}\rangle$:
  \begin{align*}
    \Gcal^{(a)}_k &: (\sigma_1,\dots,\sigma_k,\sigma_k,\dots,\sigma_n),\
      (a_1,\dots,a_{k-1},a,a_k,\dots,a_n),\\
    \Gcal^{(a),L}_{k,l} &: (\sigma_1,\dots,\sigma_k,\sigma_l,\dots,\sigma_n),\
      (a_1,\dots,a_{k-1},a,a_l,\dots,a_n),\\
    \Gcal^{(a),R}_{k,l} &: (\sigma_k,\dots,\sigma_l),\ (a_k,\dots,a_{l-1},a).
  \end{align*}
  Only the index data is formalized so far; the Green's functions are not.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Lengths under cut and glue]\label{lem:loops-length}
  \lean{RBM.LoopIdx.length_cutGlue, RBM.LoopIdx.length_cutGlueL,
    RBM.LoopIdx.length_cutGlueR, RBM.LoopIdx.length_cutGlueL_add_length_cutGlueR,
    RBM.LoopIdx.length_cutGlueL_le, RBM.LoopIdx.length_cutGlueR_le,
    RBM.LoopIdx.length_cutGlueR_one}
  \leanok\uses{def:loops}
  For $1\le k<l\le n$ the new lengths are $n+1$, $k+n-l+1$ and $l-k+1$.  The two
  chains together have length $n+2$, and each has length at most $n$, with equality
  for the right loop at $k=1$, $l=n$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{def:loops}
  Count the entries of the lists.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Primitive equation, Definition 2.12]\label{def:primitive}
  \lean{RBM.primRhs, RBM.primInit, RBM.IsPrimitive}\leanok
  \uses{def:loops, def:SB}
  $K$ solves
  \[ \tfrac{d}{dt}K_{t,\sigma,a} = W\sum_{1\le k<l\le n}\sum_{a,b}
     (\Gcal^{(a),L}_{k,l}\circ K_{t,\sigma,a})\,\SB_{ab}\,(\Gcal^{(b),R}_{k,l}\circ K_{t,\sigma,a}) \]
  with $K_{0,\sigma,a}=W^{-n+1}\prod_k m(\sigma_k)\,\mathbf 1(a_1=\dots=a_n)$, and
  $K_{t,\pm,a}=m(\pm)$ for $n=1$.  The function $m$ of (2.42) is a parameter.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Index check at $n=2$, (2.55)]\label{eq:2.55}
  \lean{RBM.primRhs_two}\leanok\uses{def:primitive, lem:SB-basic}
  At $n=2$ the right-hand side of (2.48) is
  $W\sum_{a,b}K_{t,\sigma,(a_1,a)}\SB_{ab}K_{t,\sigma,(b,a_2)}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{def:primitive, lem:SB-basic}
  Only $k=1$, $l=2$ contributes; the operators give
  $\sum_{a,b}K_{\sigma,(a,a_2)}\SB_{ab}K_{\sigma,(a_1,b)}$, and swapping $a\leftrightarrow b$
  with $\SB$ symmetric gives (2.55).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Example 2.15, (2.57)--(2.58)]\label{ex:2.15}
  \lean{RBM.kTwo, RBM.hasDerivAt_kTwo, RBM.kTwo_zero, RBM.hasDerivAt_kTwoLoop}\leanok
  \uses{eq:2.55, lem:Theta-deriv}
  $K_{t,\sigma,(a_1,a_2)}=W^{-1}m_1m_2(\Th_{t m_1m_2})_{a_1a_2}$ solves (2.55) wherever
  $|t\,m_1m_2|<1$, and at $t=0$ equals the initial value of Definition 2.12.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{eq:2.55, lem:Theta-deriv}
  By (2.51) and the chain rule the derivative is $W^{-1}(m_1m_2)^2(\Th\SB\Th)_{a_1a_2}$,
  which is the double sum in (2.55); $\Th_0=1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[$G$-loops, Definition 2.9 and (2.41)]\label{def:gloop}
  \lean{RBM.Gsig, RBM.Gsig_conjTranspose, RBM.green_sub_green, RBM.green_sub_green_conj,
    RBM.trace_green_sub_trace_green_conj, RBM.gloopProd, RBM.gloop,
    RBM.gloopProd_append, RBM.gloop_rotate, RBM.sum_gloop_head, RBM.gloop_two,
    RBM.green_sub_green_conj', RBM.trace_green_sub_trace_green_conj',
    RBM.sum_gloop_two_ward, RBM.sum_block_ite, RBM.gloop_two_plus_minus,
    RBM.gloop_two_plus_minus_blocks, RBM.gloop_two_plus_minus_nonneg,
    RBM.im_green_apply_eq_mul_sum_normSq, RBM.normSq_green_le_im_green}
  \leanok\uses{def:model, def:loops, eq:2.10}
  For a fixed deterministic Hermitian $H$, $G(+) = (H-z)^{-1}$, $G(-) = (H-\bar z)^{-1}$ and
  \[ \mathcal L_{\sigma,\mathbf a} = \Bigl\langle \prod_i G(\sigma_i) E_{a_i} \Bigr\rangle ,
     \qquad \langle A\rangle = \operatorname{Tr} A . \]
  Proved here: $G(\sigma)^\dagger = G(-\sigma)$; the resolvent identity
  $G(z)-G(w) = (z-w)G(z)G(w)$, which is the source of every Ward identity downstream;
  and invariance of the loop under rotating its index data (trace cyclicity), which is
  the algebraic content of the word ``loop''.  Nothing here is probabilistic: the
  expectation and the It\^o calculus act on top of this layer.

  Also proved: the Ward identity $G(z)-G(\bar z) = 2i\eta\,G(z)G(\bar z)$ and its traced
  form $\operatorname{Tr}(G(z)A)-\operatorname{Tr}(G(\bar z)A) = 2i\eta\operatorname{Tr}(G(z)G(\bar z)A)$;
  and the one-index sum rule $\sum_b \mathcal L_{\sigma,(b,\mathbf a)} = W^{-1}\langle G(\sigma_1)\cdots\rangle$,
  which is $\sum_a E_a = W^{-1}I$ and is the first step of the paper's Ward identity for loops.
  Putting the two together gives the Ward identity for the $2$-loop,
  \[ 2i\eta\sum_b \mathcal L_{(+,-),(a,b)}
     = W^{-1}\bigl(\operatorname{Tr}(G(z)E_a)-\operatorname{Tr}(G(\bar z)E_a)\bigr) , \]
  a quadratic quantity on the left and a linear one on the right --- the identity that
  makes the local law self-improving.

  Finally, the $(+,-)$ $2$-loop is computed in closed form,
  \[ \mathcal L_{(+,-),(a,b)} = W^{-2}\sum_{\beta,\alpha}
     \bigl|G_{(b,\beta),(a,\alpha)}\bigr|^2 \ \ge 0 , \]
  which is the bridge to delocalization: the loop hierarchy evolves exactly the mass of
  the Green's function between two blocks.  Specialising the same identity to a single
  column gives the Ward identity in the form used everywhere,
  $\operatorname{Im}G_{qq} = \eta\sum_p |G_{pq}|^2$, hence
  $\eta|G_{pq}|^2 \le \operatorname{Im}G_{qq}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[The initial loops, (2.67)]\label{eq:2.67}
  \lean{RBM.green_zero, RBM.green_zero_eq_smul, RBM.zt_zero, RBM.conj_zt_zero, RBM.mSigma_mul,
    RBM.Gsig_zero_zt_zero, RBM.Gsig_zero_zt_zero_apply, RBM.Gsig_zero_zt_zero_sub, RBM.trace_Eblk,
    RBM.prod_map_Eblk_cons, RBM.gloopProd_of_Gsig_eq_smul, RBM.forall_mem_cons_eq_iff,
    RBM.gloop_zero_zt_zero, RBM.gloop_zero_zt_zero_eq_Kgen, RBM.gloop_zero_zt_zero_eq_of_isPrimitive}
  \leanok\uses{def:gloop, def:primitive, lem:2.8, lem:model}
  At $t=0$ ($H_0=0$, $z_0=E+m^{(E)}$): $G_0(\sigma)=m(\sigma)I$ and
  $L_{0,\sigma,a}=W^{-n+1}\prod_k m(\sigma_k)\,\mathbf 1(a_1=\dots=a_n)=K_{0,\sigma,a}$, with no
  hypotheses --- the base case of the induction of \S2.7.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok
  $G_0=(-z_0)^{-1}I$ and $-1/(E+m)=m$; then $E_aE_b=\delta_{ab}W^{-1}E_a$ and $\operatorname{Tr}E_a=1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[$G$-chains, Appendix A]\label{def:gchain}
  \lean{RBM.gchain, RBM.gchain_append_one, RBM.gchain_conjTranspose,
    RBM.gchain_mul_Eblk, RBM.trace_gchain_mul_Eblk}
  \leanok\uses{def:gloop}
  $C_{\boldsymbol\tau,\mathbf a} = G(\tau_1)E_{a_1}G(\tau_2)\cdots E_{a_{n-1}}G(\tau_n)$
  (Definition A.1).  Two algebraic facts, both deterministic:
  $\langle C_{\boldsymbol\tau,\mathbf a}E_b\rangle = \mathcal L_{\boldsymbol\tau,\mathbf a b}$
  --- a chain closed by one $E$ is a loop --- and $C^\dagger$ is again a chain, with the
  charges reversed and flipped.  \emph{A chain conjugates to a chain}; the same computation
  for a loop produces a cyclic shift as well, which is why the loop version is the awkward
  statement.  The paper notes these results are not used in the main theorem.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Classification of canonical partitions, Lemma 3.2]\label{lem:3.2}
  \lean{RBM.IsCanonicalTree, RBM.Sep, RBM.OnRegion, RBM.Paired, RBM.FGamma, RBM.exists_leaf_sep, RBM.leafSide_eq_Ico, RBM.interval_of_two_cycChange, RBM.edge_unique_of_paired, RBM.crossingFree_FGamma, RBM.FGamma_mem_TSP, RBM.figure_four, RBM.canonGraph, RBM.FGamma_canonGraph, RBM.isCanonicalTree_canonGraph, RBM.mem_TSP_iff_exists_isCanonicalTree, RBM.canonIso, RBM.exists_iso_of_FGamma_eq}
  \leanok\uses{def:loops}
  Canonical partitions of the oriented $n$-gon are in
  bijection with crossing-free subsets of $\mathbb{Z}_n^{\mathrm{off}}$.

  \emph{Formalized} (for $n\ge3$; at $n=2$ the model degenerates, the correct tree being a single
  edge while the construction would give an internal vertex of degree 2).  The one modelling step, stated here and in the file header: a
  canonical partition of $P_a$ \emph{is} its tree $\Gamma$ of Part~III -- a finite leaf-labelled
  tree whose leaves are exactly $a_0,\dots,a_{n-1}$, whose internal vertices have degree $\ge3$,
  and each of whose edges lies on exactly two of the $n$ region paths.  On that model all of
  (0), (1), realization and uniqueness are theorems: $F(\Gamma)$ is crossing-free, every
  crossing-free $F$ is $F(\Gamma)$ for an explicit $\Gamma$ built from the laminar encoding, and
  $F(\Gamma)=F(\Gamma')$ forces a label-preserving isomorphism.  So the image is exactly
  $\mathcal{T}_{SP}\,n$ and Definition~\ref{def:TSP} is now a theorem rather than a convention.
  The model was checked outside Lean by exhaustive enumeration for $n\le7$: the tree counts are
  $1,3,11,45,197$, matching $|\mathcal{T}_{SP}\,n|$, and Figure 4's tree gives the displayed pairs.
\end{lemma}

\begin{definition}[$\mathcal{T}_{SP}$ via Lemma 3.2]\label{def:TSP}
  \lean{RBM.IsDiag, RBM.diagonals, RBM.Crossing, RBM.CrossingFree, RBM.TSP}\leanok
  \uses{lem:3.2}
  Vertices $0,\dots,n-1$ (the paper's $1,\dots,n$ shifted).  A diagonal is $(i,j)$ with
  $i<j$ not adjacent mod $n$; $(i,j)$ and $(k,l)$ cross if $i<k<j<l$ or $k<i<l<j$.
  $\mathcal{T}_{SP}(n)$ is the set of crossing-free sets of diagonals.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Small polygons, Figure 6]\label{lem:TSP-small}
  \lean{RBM.TSP_three, RBM.TSP_four, RBM.card_TSP_five, RBM.card_TSP_six}\leanok
  \uses{def:TSP}
  $\mathcal{T}_{SP}(3)=\{\emptyset\}$, $\mathcal{T}_{SP}(4)=\{\emptyset,\{(0,2)\},\{(1,3)\}\}$,
  and $|\mathcal{T}_{SP}(5)|=11$, $|\mathcal{T}_{SP}(6)|=45$ (small Schr\"oder numbers).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok
  By \texttt{decide}.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Tree values: star graph and $n=4$, Definition 3.3]\label{def:3.3}
  \lean{RBM.thetaEdge, RBM.starGamma, RBM.gammaFour, RBM.splitGamma₀₂, RBM.splitGamma₁₃}
  \leanok\uses{def:TSP, def:Theta}
  The boundary edge at $a_i$ lies between $R_i$ and $R_{i+1}$ and carries
  $\Th_{t m_i m_{i+1}}$; an internal edge between $R_k$, $R_l$ carries $\Th_{tm_km_l}-1$.
  The star value is $\sum_b\prod_i(\Th_{tm_im_{i+1}})_{a_ib}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[$n=4$ display and the three trees]\label{lem:tree-four}
  \lean{RBM.gammaFour_eq}\leanok\uses{def:3.3, lem:TSP-small}
  The $n=4$ display after Figure 6 (with boundary factors $\Th_{tm_im_{i+1}}$) equals the sum
  of the values of the three trees of $\mathcal T_{SP}(4)=\{\emptyset,\{(0,2)\},\{(1,3)\}\}$.
  The display as printed uses $\Th_{tm_{i-1}m_i}$; only the corrected indices solve (2.48)
  (checked numerically at $n=3,4$).  See \texttt{docs/paper-deltas.md}.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{def:3.3}
  Collapse the Kronecker deltas.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The case $n=2$]\label{lem:tree-two}
  \lean{RBM.starGamma_two, RBM.not_hasDerivAt_starK_two, RBM.kTwo_eq_edge}\leanok
  \uses{def:3.3, ex:2.15}
  For $n=2$ the star formula gives $(\Th^2)_{a_1a_2}$, which does not solve (2.55): at $t=0$
  its derivative is twice the right-hand side.  The tree of the $2$-gon is the single edge
  $a_1$--$a_2$, which gives Example 2.15.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{def:3.3, ex:2.15}
  Differentiate $\sum_b\Th_{a_1b}\Th_{ba_2}$ at $t=0$ with $\Th_0=1$ and compare.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Index check at $n=3$, Example 2.16]\label{eq:2.16-index}
  \lean{RBM.primRhs_three, RBM.primRhs_three_paper, RBM.kTwo_rotate}\leanok
  \uses{def:primitive, ex:2.15}
  At $n=3$ the right-hand side of (2.48) has the three terms $(k,l)=(1,2),(1,3),(2,3)$.
  The wrap-around term $(1,3)$ has short chain $K_{(\sigma_1,\sigma_3),(b,a_3)}$; the paper
  writes $K_{(\sigma_3,\sigma_1),(a_3,b)}$, the same $2$-loop read from the other end.  They
  agree for rotation-invariant $K$, in particular for (2.57).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{def:primitive, ex:2.15}
  Evaluate the cut-and-glue operators; swap dummy indices using $\SB$ symmetric; for (2.57)
  use $\Th$ symmetric.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Tree representation at $n=3$, Example 2.16]\label{ex:2.16}
  \lean{RBM.rhs_kTwo_left, RBM.rhs_kTwo_right, RBM.kThree, RBM.kThree_eq_starGamma,
    RBM.hasDerivAt_thetaEdge, RBM.hasDerivAt_kThree, RBM.kThree_zero, RBM.kLoop3,
    RBM.hasDerivAt_kLoop3, RBM.hasDerivAt_kLoop3_mSigma, RBM.kLoop3_zero}\leanok
  \uses{eq:2.16-index, def:3.3, lem:TSP-small, lem:Theta-deriv}
  With $T_{SP}(3)=\{\emptyset\}$, the star value
  \[ K_{t,\sigma,a}=W^{-2}m_1m_2m_3\sum_b(\Th_{tm_1m_2})_{a_1b}(\Th_{tm_2m_3})_{a_2b}
     (\Th_{tm_3m_1})_{a_3b} \]
  solves (2.48) at $n=3$, the $2$-loops being (2.57), and equals the initial value of
  Definition 2.12 at $t=0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{eq:2.16-index, def:3.3, lem:TSP-small, lem:Theta-deriv}
  Substituting (2.57) turns the three terms into
  $\sum_x(m_im_{i+1}\Th_{tm_im_{i+1}}\SB)_{a_ix}K(\dots,x,\dots)$.  Differentiating the star
  by the product rule, the edge at $a_i$ gives by (2.51)
  $m_im_{i+1}(\Th\SB\Th)_{a_ib}$, and $\sum_x M_{ax}\sum_bN_{xb}g_b=\sum_b(MN)_{ab}g_b$
  matches the terms one to one.  At $t=0$, $\Th_0=1$ collapses the sum to
  $\mathbf 1(a_1=a_2=a_3)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[General tree value, Definition 3.3]\label{def:3.3-general}
  \lean{RBM.polyVal, RBM.treeVal, RBM.treeSum, RBM.diagList, RBM.noncrossing_split,
    RBM.isDiag_split_lt}
  \leanok\uses{def:3.3, def:TSP}
  $\Gamma_F$ is defined by recursion on $F$: for $F=\emptyset$ the star; otherwise the
  lexicographically first diagonal $(i,j)$ splits the polygon into regions $i,\dots,j$ and
  $j,\dots,n-1,0,\dots,i$, joined by one internal edge $\Th_{tm_im_j}-1$, and the remaining
  pairs go to the side containing them (a non-crossing pair lies on one side).  Both sides
  are smaller since a diagonal is not adjacent.  For $n\le2$ the tree is the single edge.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Acceptance at $n=4$; Lemma 3.4 at $n=2$]\label{lem:tree-accept}
  \lean{RBM.treeSum_four, RBM.kTwo_eq_treeSum}\leanok
  \uses{def:3.3-general, lem:tree-four, lem:tree-two}
  $\sum_{F\in\mathcal T_{SP}(4)}\Gamma_F$ is the $n=4$ display, and at $n=2$
  $m_\sigma W^{-1}\sum_\Gamma\Gamma = K$ of Example 2.15.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{def:3.3-general, lem:tree-four, lem:tree-two}
  Unfold the recursion on $\emptyset$, $\{(0,2)\}$, $\{(1,3)\}$; the last needs $\Th$ symmetric.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniqueness for (2.48), Definition 2.12]\label{lem:unique}
  \lean{RBM.LoopIdx.length_cutGlueR_eq_two, RBM.LoopIdx.length_cutGlueL_eq_two,
    RBM.eq_on_level, RBM.isPrimitive_unique}\leanok
  \uses{def:primitive, def:loops}
  If one chain of a term of (2.48) at length $n$ has length $n$, the other has length $2$.
  Consequently two solutions of Definition 2.12 on $[0,T_0]$ whose $2$-loops are bounded
  agree on every loop.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{def:primitive, def:loops}
  The chain lengths lie in $[2,n]$ and add up to $n+2$.  Induct on $n$: if two solutions
  agree below length $n$, each term of the difference is
  $(X-X')sY+X's(Y-Y')$, where a difference vanishes unless its chain has length $n$, and
  then the other factor is a bounded $2$-loop.  So $\|D'\|\le C\|D\|$ with $D(0)=0$, and
  Gr\"onwall gives $D\equiv0$.  For $n=2$ this is the Lipschitz bound of the Riccati
  right-hand side; for $n\ge3$ the equation is linear.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Lemma 3.4 for $n\le4$]\label{lem:3.4-small}
  \lean{RBM.primRhs_four, RBM.star4, RBM.spl02, RBM.spl13, RBM.trees4, RBM.sum_bilin,
    RBM.kFour, RBM.kFour_eq_treeSum, RBM.hasDerivAt_kFour, RBM.term13_eq, RBM.term24_eq,
    RBM.kFour_zero, RBM.kLoop4, RBM.hasDerivAt_kLoop4, RBM.kThree_eq_treeSum,
    RBM.eq_kLoop4_of_isPrimitive, RBM.treeRep_of_isPrimitive}\leanok
  \uses{ex:2.16, lem:tree-accept, lem:unique, lem:Theta-deriv, lem:Theta-bounds}
  If $K$ solves Definition 2.12 on $[0,T_0]$, $T_0<1$, with bounded $2$-loops and $|m|\le1$,
  then (3.5) holds for every loop of length $n\in\{2,3,4\}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{ex:2.16, lem:tree-accept, lem:unique, lem:Theta-deriv, lem:Theta-bounds}
  At $n=4$ the six terms of (2.48) match the six edges of the trees of $T_{SP}(4)$: each of
  the four boundary rows occurs in all three trees, and differentiating it gives
  $\sum_x(m_im_{i+1}\Th\SB)_{a_ix}K(\dots x\dots)$, the term with the $2$-chain
  $(\sigma_i,\sigma_{i+1})$; the internal edge $\Th-1$ of each split tree differentiates to
  $m\,m'\,\Th\SB\Th$, which factors the tree into $K_3\,\SB\,K_3$, the term with two $3$-chains.
  Then compare level by level with Lemma~\ref{lem:unique}.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Pivot-free tree value]\label{def:tree-general}
  \lean{RBM.wholeP, RBM.InArc, RBM.ArcLe, RBM.nodes, RBM.leafPar, RBM.nodePar, RBM.treeValW,
    RBM.treeValG, RBM.gval, RBM.Kn, RBM.Kgen}\leanok
  \uses{def:TSP, def:3.3}
  A crossing-free set $F$ of diagonals is a laminar family of vertex arcs ($(i,j)\mapsto
  \{i,\dots,j-1\}$), rooted at the last vertex; the internal vertices of the tree are
  $F\cup\{\text{whole}\}$, each hanging on its smallest strict container.  The tree value sums
  over labels of all internal vertices at once, with leaf edges $\Th_{tm_vm_{v+1}}$ and
  internal edges $\Th_{tm_im_j}-1$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Cutting a tree along an edge]\label{lem:tree-cut}
  \lean{RBM.gval_congr, RBM.gval_split, RBM.nodes_laminar, RBM.leafPar_spec, RBM.nodePar_spec,
    RBM.treeValW_cut, RBM.gval_in_eq, RBM.gval_out_eq, RBM.sum_cut, RBM.treeValW_internal_cut}
  \leanok
  \uses{def:tree-general}
  Putting $\Th_J\SB\Th_J$ on the internal edge $J$ factors the tree into a tree on the inside
  polygon (root label $u$) and one on the outside polygon ($J$ collapsed to a glue vertex,
  label $w$), joined by $\SB_{uw}$; and $F\mapsto(F_{\rm out},F_{\rm in})$ is a bijection from
  $\{F\in T_{SP}(n):J\in F\}$ onto $T_{SP}(\text{outside})\times T_{SP}(\text{inside})$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{def:tree-general}
  Laminarity puts every node and leaf on one side of $J$; split the label sum (Fubini) and
  transport each side by explicit bijections that preserve arc containment, hence parents.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Tree representation of $\Kcal$, Lemma 3.4]\label{lem:3.4}
  \lean{RBM.hasDerivAt_Kn, RBM.leaf_term, RBM.internal_term, RBM.sum_pairs, RBM.leaf_pair_term,
    RBM.root_pair_term, RBM.diag_pair_term, RBM.hasDerivAt_Kgen, RBM.hasDerivAt_Kgen_all,
    RBM.Kgen_zero, RBM.isPrimitive_Kgen, RBM.eq_Kgen_of_isPrimitive, RBM.treeRep_general}\leanok
  \uses{def:TSP, def:primitive, lem:Theta-deriv, lem:unique, lem:tree-cut, lem:3.4-small}
  If $K$ solves Definition 2.12 on $[0,T_0]$, $T_0<1$, $|m|\le1$, with bounded $2$-loops, then
  for every $n\ge3$,
  $K_{t,\sigma,a}=m_\sigma W^{-n+1}\sum_{\Gamma\in T_{SP}(P_a)}\Gamma_a(t,\sigma)$ (3.5).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{def:TSP, def:primitive, lem:Theta-deriv, lem:unique, lem:tree-cut}
  Root the tree at the last vertex.  Then every edge $e$ of a tree $\Gamma$ corresponds to
  exactly one pair $(k,l)$: cutting at $e$ leaves the part containing the root on the left
  chain and the cut-off subtree on the right chain, rooted at the glue vertex.  Leaf edges give
  the pairs $(k,k+1)$, the root edge $(1,n)$, internal edges their diagonals.  Differentiating
  the tree sum edge by edge therefore reproduces (2.48) term by term; at $t=0$ only the star
  survives.  So the tree sum solves Definition 2.12, and Lemma~\ref{lem:unique} identifies it
  with $K$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Pure loop $\Kcal$, Corollary 3.5]\label{cor:3.5}
  \lean{RBM.sum_pow_zdist_le, RBM.Cor35.chain, RBM.Cor35.dist_bounds,
    RBM.Cor35.norm_treeValW_le, RBM.Cor35.norm_Theta_apply_le_of_gap,
    RBM.Cor35.norm_thetaEdge_le, RBM.Cor35.gap_of_le, RBM.cor35Const, RBM.cor35Rate,
    RBM.cor35, RBM.cor35_two}\leanok
  \uses{lem:3.4, lem:Theta-decay-complex}
  Under the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:3.4}, if $\sigma=(+,\dots,+)$ and
  $|1-tm(+)^2|\ge\delta>0$ on $[0,T_0]$, then
  $|K_{t,\sigma,a}|\le C_{n,\delta}\,e^{-c_\delta\|a_i-a_j\|}$ for all $i,j$ (3.6), with
  $C_{n,\delta}$, $c_\delta=c_0\sqrt\delta/4$ independent of $L$, $W$, $t$, $a$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:3.4, lem:Theta-decay-complex}
  All edges are $\Theta_\xi$ or $\Theta_\xi-1$ with $\xi=tm(+)^2$; by (2.52) and the gap every
  entry is $\le B e^{-\kappa\|x-y\|}$.  In a tree the distance from any node to the root, and
  between any two leaves (up to a factor $2$), is at most the total edge length $D$.  Split
  $e^{-\kappa D}=e^{-\kappa D/2}e^{-\kappa D/2}$: the first factor gives the decay, the second
  dominates $\prod_\nu e^{-\kappa\|b_\nu-a_n\|/(2n^2)}$, whose label sum is
  $\bigl(\sum_x e^{-\lambda\|x\|}\bigr)^{\#\text{nodes}}$, uniformly bounded in $L$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Long edges and layers, Definition 3.8]\label{def:3.8}
  \lean{RBM.Flong, RBM.TSPlong, RBM.Flong_subset_diagonals, RBM.TSPlong_eq_empty,
    RBM.disjoint_TSPlong, RBM.biUnion_TSPlong, RBM.sum_TSPlong}\leanok
  \uses{def:TSP}
  For $F\in T_{SP}(n)$, $F_{\rm long}(F,\sigma)=\{\{i,j\}\in F:\sigma_i\ne\sigma_j\}$; for
  $\pi\subset Z^{\rm off}_n$, $T_{SP}(P_a,\sigma,\pi)=\{F: F_{\rm long}(F,\sigma)=\pi\}$.  These
  sets partition $T_{SP}(n)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[$K^{(\pi)}$ and $\Sigma^{(\pi)}$, Definition 3.9]\label{def:3.9}
  \lean{RBM.Kpi, RBM.Kn_eq_sum_Kpi, RBM.K_eq_sum_Kpi, RBM.selfW, RBM.treeValW_eq_sum_selfW,
    RBM.selfW_empty, RBM.selfE, RBM.SigmaPi, RBM.treeValG_eq_sum_selfE,
    RBM.Kpi_eq_sum_SigmaPi}\leanok
  \uses{def:3.8, def:tree-general, lem:3.4}
  $K^{(\pi)}_{t,\sigma,a}=\sum_{\Gamma_a\in T_{SP}(P_a,\sigma,\pi)}\Gamma_a(t,\sigma)$ (3.40), so
  $K_{t,\sigma,a}=W^{-n+1}m_\sigma\sum_\pi K^{(\pi)}_{t,\sigma,a}$ for $n\ge3$ (3.41).  The
  self-energy $\Sigma^{(\pi)}(t,\sigma,d)$ removes the boundary edges: every internal vertex is
  labelled, with $d_v$ equal to the label of the vertex the boundary edge at $a_v$ ends on.  Then
  $K^{(\pi)}_{t,\sigma,a}=\sum_d\Sigma^{(\pi)}(t,\sigma,d)\prod_i(\Theta_{tm_im_{i+1}})_{a_id_i}$
  (3.42).
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Cyclic invariance of $\Kcal$]\label{lem:K-cyclic}
  \lean{RBM.LoopIdx.rot, RBM.primRhs_rot, RBM.primInit_rot, RBM.rot_eq_on_level,
    RBM.isPrimitive_rot, RBM.Kgen_rot}\leanok
  \uses{def:primitive, def:loops}
  A solution of Definition 2.12 on $[0,T_0]$ with bounded $2$-loops satisfies
  $K_{t,(\sigma_2,\dots,\sigma_n,\sigma_1),(a_2,\dots,a_n,a_1)}=K_{t,\sigma,a}$.
  The proof of Lemma 3.6 (Step 4) uses this (``cyclicity'') without proof.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{def:primitive, def:loops}
  The cut $(k,l)$ of the rotated loop is the cut $(k+1,l+1)$ of the original, or for $l=n$
  the cut $(1,k+1)$ with the chains swapped.  So on loops of length $n$, $K\circ\mathrm{rot}-K$
  satisfies a linear equation with $2$-loop coefficients and zero initial value; Gr\"onwall
  and induction on $n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Ward identity for $\Kcal$ at $n=2,3$, Lemma 3.6 Step 1]\label{lem:3.6-small}
  \lean{RBM.etaT, RBM.ward_two, RBM.mul_Theta_sub_mul_Theta, RBM.ward_three,
    RBM.wardC, RBM.ward_two_of_isPrimitive}\leanok
  \uses{ex:2.15, lem:Theta-rowsum, lem:2.8}
  With $m=m^{(E)}$, $\eta_t=(1-t)\operatorname{Im}m$ and $\sigma_1=+$, $\sigma_n=-$:
  $\sum_{a_n}K_{t,\sigma,a}=(K_{t,\sigma^+,a/a_n}-K_{t,\sigma^-,a/a_n})/(2Wi\eta_t)$ for the
  explicit loops at $n=2$ ((3.16)) and $n=3$.  At $n=2$ it holds for every solution of
  Definition 2.12 with bounded $2$-loops.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{ex:2.15, lem:Theta-rowsum, lem:2.8}
  $n=2$: $|m|=1$ and the row sums of $\Th$ give $W^{-1}(1-t)^{-1}$ on both sides.
  $n=3$: with $A=\Th_{tmq}$, $B=\Th_{t\bar mq}$, $mA-\bar mB=(m-\bar m)AB$, because the
  $\SB$ terms of $mB^{-1}-\bar mA^{-1}$ cancel.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Ward identity for $\Kcal$, Lemma 3.6, (3.13)]\label{lem:3.6}
  \lean{RBM.ward_of_isPrimitive, RBM.sum_fullLoop_eq, RBM.ward_rhs_identity, RBM.ward_level,
    RBM.wD_primInit, RBM.wardKappa, RBM.wStar, RBM.wD, RBM.ward_Kgen}\leanok
  \uses{def:primitive, lem:Theta-rowsum, lem:3.6-small, lem:K-cyclic, lem:2.8}
  For a solution of Definition 2.12 with $m=m^{(E)}$ on $[0,T_0]$, $T_0<1$, with bounded
  $2$-loops, and $\sigma_1=+$, $\sigma_n=-$:
  $\sum_{a_n}K_{t,\sigma,a}=(K_{t,\sigma^+,a/a_n}-K_{t,\sigma^-,a/a_n})/(2Wi\eta_t)$.
  By Lemma 3.4 the primitive loop itself is such a solution, so the identity holds for it for
  every $0\le t<1$ without further hypotheses.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok
  \uses{def:primitive, lem:Theta-rowsum, lem:3.6-small, lem:K-cyclic, lem:2.8}
  The defect $D$ satisfies (3.18): for every cut $(k,l)$ with $l\le n-1$ the left chain of the
  full loop is again a full loop and that of $\sigma^\pm$ is its $\sigma^\pm$, so the
  $a_n$-sum gives $D$ one level down plus the matching $\sigma^\pm$ term; the cuts $(1,m)$ and
  $(m,n)$ cancel for every $m$ by cyclic invariance; the cut $(1,n)$ and
  $\partial_t\eta_t^{-1}=\eta_t^{-1}/(1-t)$ give $D/(1-t)$.  $D_0=0$; Gr\"onwall and induction
  on $n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Translation invariance of $\Kcal$]\label{lem:K-shift}
  \lean{RBM.LoopIdx.shift, RBM.primRhs_shift, RBM.primInit_shift, RBM.isPrimitive_shift,
    RBM.Kgen_shift}\leanok
  \uses{def:primitive, lem:SB-basic}
  $K_{t,\sigma,a+c}=K_{t,\sigma,a}$; the paper uses this ``by definition of $K$''.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{def:primitive, lem:SB-basic}
  Shifting the labels commutes with cut and glue and $\SB$ is translation invariant, so
  $K\circ\mathrm{shift}$ solves (2.48) with the same initial value; uniqueness.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Corollary 3.7, (3.14)--(3.15)]\label{cor:3.7}
  \lean{RBM.allSum, RBM.totalSum, RBM.pureNorm, RBM.totalSum_ward, RBM.totalSum_rotate,
    RBM.exists_rotate_true_false, RBM.norm_totalSum_le, RBM.partialSum_eq,
    RBM.cor37_reduction, RBM.cor37, RBM.norm_Kgen_pure_le, RBM.norm_totalSum_pure_two,
    RBM.norm_totalSum_pure_le, RBM.cor37_bulk}\leanok
  \uses{lem:3.6, lem:K-cyclic, lem:K-shift, cor:3.5}
  (3.15): $\bigl|\sum_{a_2,\dots,a_n}K_{t,\sigma,a}\bigr|\le L^{-1}2^{n-1}
  \sum_{m=1}^n(2W\eta_t)^{-(n-m)}P_m$, with $P_m$ the pure loops of length $m$.
  For $|E|\le 2-k$ the pure loops satisfy $L^{-1}P_m\le C_m(k)W^{-(m-1)}$ (Corollary 3.5 with the
  $W$-power kept, uniformly in $t$), hence (3.14):
  $\bigl|\sum_{a_2,\dots,a_n}K_{t,\sigma,a}\bigr|\le C_n(k)(W\eta_t)^{-n+1}$ for all $0\le t<1$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:3.6, lem:K-cyclic, lem:K-shift}
  By translation invariance the sum over $a_2,\dots,a_n$ is $L^{-1}$ times the sum over all
  labels.  A mixed charge list has a cyclic pair $(-,+)$; rotating it to the seam, (3.13)
  sums out the last label at the cost $(2W\eta_t)^{-1}$ and two loops one shorter.  Iterate
  down to pure loops.  These are bounded by the tree representation: pairwise decay
  $e^{-c\|a_i-a_j\|}$ with gap $|1-tm^2|\ge\sqrt k$ gives a product bound over the labels, whose
  sum is $O(L)$; for $m=2$ the sum is exactly $LW^{-1}m^2(1-tm^2)^{-1}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Symmetry and sum zero, Lemma 3.10]\label{lem:3.10}
  \lean{RBM.selfW_add_const, RBM.selfW_neg, RBM.SigmaPi_add_const, RBM.SigmaPi_neg,
    RBM.sum_Kpi_eq, RBM.sum_out, RBM.treeZ, RBM.treeZ_peel, RBM.treeZ_eq, RBM.sum_selfW,
    RBM.Qlayer, RBM.Alayer, RBM.sum_SigmaPi, RBM.sum_Kpi_closed, RBM.sigmaIn, RBM.sigmaOut,
    RBM.prod_cut, RBM.Flong_FOut, RBM.Flong_FIn, RBM.Flong_eq_iff_cut, RBM.Qlayer_cut,
    RBM.prod_leaves_cut, RBM.Alayer_cut, RBM.norm_sum_Alayer_le, RBM.exists_innermost,
    RBM.norm_Alayer_le, RBM.sum_zero}\leanok
  \uses{def:3.9, lem:3.4, lem:tree-cut, cor:3.7}
  (1) For every $\sigma$ and $\pi$, $\Sigma^{(\pi)}(t,\sigma,d)$ is translation invariant and
  $\Sigma^{(\pi)}(t,\sigma,-d)=\Sigma^{(\pi)}(t,\sigma,d)$.  (2) In the bulk, for alternating
  $\sigma$, $L^{-1}\sum_d\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}(t,\sigma,d)=O(\eta_t)$ (3.44), uniformly in $L$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{def:3.9, lem:3.4, lem:tree-cut, cor:3.7}
  Fully summed trees have a closed form: peeling childless vertices,
  $\sum_b\prod_eE_e(b_e,b_{\mathrm{par}\,e})=L\prod_er_e$ when $E_e$ has column sums $r_e$.  So
  $A(\sigma,\pi):=L^{-1}\sum_aK^{(\pi)}=\prod_v(1-\xi_v)^{-1}Q(\sigma,\pi)$ with
  $Q=\sum_{F}\prod_{e\in F}\xi_e/(1-\xi_e)=L^{-1}\sum_d\Sigma^{(\pi)}$.  (3.49) is Corollary 3.7
  with (3.41).  At an innermost long edge $J$ the cut bijection of Lemma 3.4 gives
  $A(\sigma,\pi)=t(1-t)A(\sigma_{\rm in},\emptyset)A(\sigma_{\rm out},\pi\setminus J)$, whence
  (3.50) by induction on $n$ (for $\pi=\emptyset$ subtract the other layers from (3.49)).  For
  alternating $\sigma$, $Q=(1-t)^nA=O(\eta_t)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Short range of the self-energy, (3.43)]\label{eq:3.43}
  \lean{RBM.selfW_eq_treeValW_one, RBM.norm_selfEdge_le, RBM.norm_SigmaPi_empty_le}
  \leanok\uses{def:3.8, cor:3.5}
  For $|E|\le2-k$ and $0\le t<1$: $|\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}(t,\sigma,d)|\le C e^{-c\|d_i-d_j\|}$ for
  every pair $i,j$, with $C,c$ depending only on $n$ and $k$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{cor:3.5}
  For $\pi=\emptyset$ every internal edge joins equal charges, so it is $\Theta_{tm^2}-1$, which
  decays on the scale of the bulk gap $|1-tm^2|\ge\sqrt k$.  The self-energy is the tree value
  with identity boundary edges, and the tree bound of Corollary 3.5 applies.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Discrete Taylor split on the cycle]\label{lem:taylor}
  \lean{RBM.oddPart, RBM.evenPart, RBM.lap, RBM.apply_add_eq, RBM.oddPart_neg,
    RBM.evenPart_neg, RBM.eq_natCast_or_neg, RBM.norm_oddPart_le, RBM.evenPart_natCast_eq,
    RBM.norm_evenPart_le, RBM.norm_evenPart_le_const, RBM.hitCount, RBM.sum_hitCount,
    RBM.norm_evenPart_le_split}
  \leanok
  $f(c+s) = f(c) + o(c,s) + e(c,s)$ with $o$ odd in $s$, $|o|\le\|s\|\sup|\nabla f|$, and
  $e = \tfrac12\sum_{j<\|s\|}\sum_{i<2j+1}\Delta f(c-j+i)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok
  Telescoping along the cycle, twice.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Lemma 3.11, (3.45), for $\pi=\emptyset$]\label{lem:3.11-empty}
  \lean{RBM.pinned, RBM.sum_center, RBM.polyExpSum, RBM.sum_exp_mul_pow_le,
    RBM.le_mul_prod_of_forall, RBM.sigWeightConst, RBM.sum_pinned_SigmaPi_le, RBM.taylorTerm,
    RBM.sum_taylorTerm, RBM.thetaEdge_of_ne, RBM.Kpi_empty_expand, RBM.norm_taylorTerm_le,
    RBM.norm_taylorTerm_zero_le, RBM.sum_mul_prod_le, RBM.sum_prod_taylor_le,
    RBM.sum_pinned_odd_eq_zero, RBM.sum_eq_mul_sum_pinned, RBM.sum_taylor_single_eq_zero,
    RBM.sum_taylor_zero_eq, RBM.etaT_mul_ellHat_sq_le, RBM.etaT_mul_ellHat_le,
    RBM.sigWeightConst_nonneg, RBM.norm_Kpi_empty_alt_le, RBM.norm_thetaEdge_same_le,
    RBM.norm_Kpi_empty_short_le, RBM.norm_Kpi_empty_le}
  \leanok\uses{lem:3.10, eq:3.43, lem:long-diff, lem:taylor, lem:3.35}
  For $|E|\le2-k$ there is $C=C(n,k)$ with
  $|K^{(\emptyset)}_{t,\sigma,a}|\le C(\eta_t\hat\ell(t))^{-n+1}$ for all $\sigma$, $a$,
  $0<t<1$, $L\ge3$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:3.10, eq:3.43, lem:long-diff, lem:taylor}
  Centre at $d_1$: $K^{(\emptyset)}_a=\sum_c\sum_{s_1=0}\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}(s)\prod_v f_v(c+s_v)$.
  If some boundary edge is short, sum the centre with it ($\ell^1=O(1)$) and bound the others by
  their sup.  If $\sigma$ alternates, every edge is $\Theta_t$; split each factor as
  $f(c)+o+e$ and multiply out.  The leading term is $O(\eta_t)$ by the sum-zero property; the
  terms with one $o$ vanish because $\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}(-s)=\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}(s)$; every other
  term gains $\eta_t$ from $|\nabla\Theta_t|\le 3/2$, $|\Delta\Theta_t|\le 24/\hat\ell$ off the
  diagonal and $\eta_t\hat\ell^2\le1$.  This replaces the paper's (3.66), whose min factor is
  not justified (paper-deltas \#22).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Bound on $\Kcal$, Lemma 3.11]\label{lem:3.11}
  \lean{RBM.treeValW_leaf_smul, RBM.treeValW_long_cut, RBM.innerId, RBM.innerKer, RBM.innerId_eq,
    RBM.sum_comp_add_const, RBM.sum_norm_oddPart_le, RBM.sum_norm_evenPart_le,
    RBM.sum_prod_taylor_le_at, RBM.sum_prod_taylor_le_noval, RBM.sum_taylor_single_eq_zero_pt,
    RBM.prod_taylor_expand, RBM.sum_norm_innerId_alt_le, RBM.sum_norm_innerId_short_le,
    RBM.sum_norm_innerId_le, RBM.aIn, RBM.aOut, RBM.Kpi_cut, RBM.sum_norm_SB_row,
    RBM.exists_uniform, RBM.norm_Kpi_le, RBM.norm_Kgen_le}
  \leanok\uses{lem:3.10, lem:3.11-empty, def:3.8, lem:tree-accept}
  For $|E|\le2-k$ and $3\le n\le N$ there is $C=C(N,k)$ such that for every layer $\pi$,
  $|K^{(\pi)}_{t,\sigma,a}|\le C(\eta_t\hat\ell(t))^{-n+1}$ (3.45) and
  $|K_{t,\sigma,a}|\le C(W\eta_t\hat\ell(t))^{-n+1}$ (3.46), uniformly in $L\ge3$ and $0<t<1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok\uses{lem:3.11-empty, lem:taylor, lem:long-diff}
  Induction on $n$; $\pi=\emptyset$ is Lemma~\ref{lem:3.11-empty}.  For $\pi\ne\emptyset$ cut at an
  innermost long edge $J$: $\Theta_t-1=(t\cdot1)S\Theta_t$ splits every tree into the inside
  polygon with an identity root leaf (the molecule $A(c_1)$ of (3.75)) and the outside polygon, a
  genuine tree of the layer $\pi\setminus J$.  Hence
  $|K^{(\pi)}|\le t\sum_u|A(u)|\cdot\sup_w|K^{(\pi')}(w)|$.  The molecule satisfies
  $\sum_u|A(u)|=O((\eta_t\hat\ell)^{-N_{in}+2})$ by the same Taylor expansion pinned at the root
  (the $\ell^1$ sum over the centre falls on a value factor, or on an odd/even piece using
  $\sum|\nabla\Theta_t|\le3\hat\ell$, $\sum|\Delta\Theta_t|\le6$), and the outside loop is bounded by
  induction.  (3.46) follows from (3.41) and at most $2^{n^2}$ layers.
\end{proof}

\chapter{Deterministic ingredients of the flow}

\begin{lemma}[Scales of the flow and the time grid, \S2.7 and p.21]\label{lem:flow-scales}
  \lean{RBM.flowScale, RBM.mE_im_nonneg, RBM.ellHat_mul_one_sub, RBM.flowScale_eq, RBM.flowScale_antitoneOn, RBM.flowScale_pos, RBM.flowScale_inv_le, RBM.gridS, RBM.gridS_zero, RBM.one_sub_gridS, RBM.gridS_lt_one, RBM.gridS_nonneg, RBM.gridS_mono, RBM.one_sub_gridS_succ, RBM.rpow_neg_pow_thirty, RBM.gridS_ratio_pow, RBM.gridS_step_2_72, RBM.gridT, RBM.gridT_zero, RBM.gridT_of_le, RBM.gridT_mono, RBM.gridT_le, RBM.rpow_neg_mul_one_sub_gridT_le, RBM.gridT_step_2_72, RBM.le_gridS_of, RBM.flowScale_ge, RBM.exists_inv_le_rpow, RBM.flowScale_inv_le_rpow, RBM.mE_im_ge, RBM.flow_grid_2_72, RBM.etaT_le, RBM.le_etaT, RBM.ellOf, RBM.ellZ, RBM.ellOf_antitone, RBM.ellOf_le_sqrt_mul, RBM.ellOf_one_sub, RBM.one_le_ellHat_of_nonneg, RBM.ellHat_le_ellZ_zt, RBM.ellZ_zt_le, RBM.sqrt_sixteen, RBM.lemma28_scales}
  \leanok\uses{lem:2.8, lem:Theta-decay}
  $A_t=W\ell_t\eta_t=W\operatorname{Im}m\cdot\min((1-t)^{1/2},L(1-t))$ is non-increasing in $t$;
  along the geometric grid $1-s_k=W^{-k\tau'}$ (truncated at $t$) the step condition (2.72)
  $A_{s_{k+1}}^{-1}\le((1-s_{k+1})/(1-s_k))^{30}$ holds; $\eta_t\asymp1-t$,
  $\ell(z_t)\asymp\ell_t$, $\ell(z)\asymp\ell_t$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok
  The two branches of the min are monotone; $\tau,\tau',n_0$ are fixed first and the grid is
  truncated at $t$ instead of solving $1-t=W^{-n_0\tau'}$.
\end{proof}

  \lean{RBM.wmass, RBM.wmass_nonneg, RBM.trace_mul_diagonal_mul_mul_diagonal, RBM.trace_mul_diagonal_mul_conjTranspose_mul_diagonal, RBM.norm_sq_trace_mul_diagonal_le, RBM.wmass_mul_diagonal_mul_le, RBM.bw, RBM.bw_nonneg, RBM.Eblk_eq_diagonal_bw, RBM.trace_mul_Eblk_mul_conjTranspose_mul_Eblk, RBM.norm_trace_mul_Eblk_mul_conjTranspose_mul_Eblk, RBM.norm_sq_trace_Eblk_le, RBM.norm_trace_Eblk_split_le, RBM.gchain_append, RBM.gloop_append_eq_trace, RBM.symIdx, RBM.gloop_symIdx, RBM.norm_sq_gloop_le_symIdx, RBM.norm_gloop_symIdx_split_le, RBM.exists_split_loopIdx, RBM.exists_split_chainIdx, RBM.loopMax, RBM.norm_gloop_le_loopMax, RBM.loopMax_le, RBM.loopMax_nonneg, RBM.norm_gloop_symIdx_le_loopMax, RBM.norm_gloop_symIdx_le_loopMax_mul, RBM.norm_gloop_le_of_symIdx_le, RBM.loopMax_two_mul_add_le, RBM.loopMax_two_mul_add_two_le, RBM.loopMax_odd_sq_le, RBM.loopXi, RBM.loopXi_le, RBM.norm_apply_le_l2_opNorm, RBM.norm_green_le, RBM.norm_Gsig_le, RBM.norm_Eblk_le, RBM.sum_bw, RBM.norm_trace_mul_Eblk_le, RBM.norm_gchain_le, RBM.norm_gloop_le_opNorm, RBM.norm_gloop_le_of_le_abs_im, RBM.re_dotProduct_mulVec_le, RBM.sum_norm_inner_sq_le_of_eigenvalues_le, RBM.trace_cfc_eq_sum, RBM.sum_norm_inner_sq_le_trace_rpow, RBM.sum_norm_inner_sq_le_trace_pow}
  \leanok\uses{def:gloop, def:gchain}
  For fixed Hermitian $H$: $|L_{\sigma,a}|\le|\operatorname{Im}z|^{-n}W^{-n+1}$ (5.2);
  $\max|L^{(2n+2)}|\le\max|L^{(2l_1)}|\max|L^{(2l_2)}|$ (5.117), hence (5.118);
  $(\max|L^{(2m+1)}|)^2\le\max|L^{(2m)}|\max|L^{(2m+2)}|$ (6.4); and Lemma 6.1,

  $\operatorname{Tr}(XE_{b'}YE_b)$; Lemma 6.1 from $\lambda_{\max}(A)\le(\operatorname{tr}A^p)^{1/p}$.
\end{proof}

  $|(U_{s,t,\sigma}A)_a|\le C_nK^n(\ell_t/\ell_s)R^n\|A\|_{\max}+(\eta_s/\eta_t)^n\delta$ (7.14),
  $R=\ell_s\eta_s/(\ell_t\eta_t)$; without the factor $\ell_t/\ell_s$ if some edge is short or $A$ is

RBM.Step6.sharpExpect_step6, RBM.lkPath, RBM.driftELK, RBM.driftEG, RBM.driftF_path_eq_add, RBM.driftE_eq_driftELK_add_driftEG, RBM.norm_primBil_lkPath_le, RBM.norm_integral_le_add_measure_compl, RBM.QuadInputs, RBM.unifDetDom_driftELK, RBM.eventually_rpow_neg_three_le_drift_target, RBM.fastDecay_integral_of_highProb, RBM.FDInputs, RBM.fastDecayHyp_driftSplit, RBM.hierarchy_driftSplit, RBM.sharpExpect_step6_driftSplit, RBM.eG_add_right, RBM.integral_eG_const_right, RBM.gloop_one_conj, RBM.lkPath_one_conj, RBM.norm_integral_lkPath_one, RBM.norm_eG_two_le, RBM.eG_gloop_eq_add, RBM.driftEG_eq_add, RBM.exists_loopDecay_Kval, RBM.EGInputs, RBM.mem_egInputs_of, RBM.unifDetDom_driftEG, RBM.driftBound_driftEG, RBM.scale_shape_check, RBM.pathwise_shape_is_one_power_weaker, RBM.sharpExpect_step6_driftEG, RBM.Gauss.Xmat_zero, RBM.Gauss.Hflow_at_zero, RBM.Gauss.mE_zero, RBM.Gauss.zt_zero_energy, RBM.Gauss.gZero, RBM.Gauss.Gsig_zero_eq_smul, RBM.Gauss.gloop_zero_one, RBM.Gauss.gloop_zero_three_const, RBM.Gauss.loopData_const_idx, RBM.Gauss.cutGlue_one_const, RBM.Gauss.cutGlue_two_const, RBM.Gauss.lkPath_zero_one, RBM.Gauss.gloop_zero_cutGlue_one, RBM.Gauss.gloop_zero_cutGlue_two, RBM.Gauss.eG_at_zero, RBM.Gauss.gZero_imag, RBM.Gauss.eG_value, RBM.Gauss.not_exists_env_eG, RBM.Gauss.hEL_gauss, RBM.Gauss.hintL2_gauss, RBM.bounds_of_boundsCore_of_sharpExpect, RBM.bounds_of_boundsCore_of_sharpExpect_eq, RBM.Gauss.sharpExpect_zero, RBM.Gauss.bounds_witness_zero}
  \leanok\uses{lem:7.3, thm:step45, def:flow-hypotheses, lem:flow-scales, thm:driftdef, lem:5.10}
  With the expected hierarchy (5.129)--(5.131), Lemma 5.15 (5.126) and the fast-decay claim as
  hypotheses: the self-consistent equation (5.128) is solved by $\Theta^{(B)}_{um^2}$, the drift
  terms satisfy (5.133)--(5.135), and the time integral (5.136) with (7.14) gives
  $|\mathbb E\,(L-K)_{u,(+,-)}|\prec(W\ell_t\eta_t)^{-3}$ uniformly in $u\in[s,t]$, i.e.\ (2.80).

  \emph{The drift must be split into two tensors; the one-tensor reduction is refuted.}  Merging
  $\mathbb E[\mathrm{primBil}(L-K,L-K)]$ and $\mathbb E[E^{(G)}]$ into one object costs nothing
  for the \emph{identity} but does cost for the \emph{size estimate}: bound pathwise, the
  $E^{(G)}$ half is exactly one factor $A=W\ell_u\eta_u$ weaker than (5.133), and the two
  ingredients that might have supplied it, $\Xi^{(L)}_{u,3}\lesssim1$ and
  $\Xi^{(L-K)}_{u,1}\lesssim1$, are both already spent.  So \texttt{driftELK} and
  \texttt{driftEG} are separate definitions and their sum is the theorem
  \texttt{driftE\_eq\_driftELK\_add\_driftEG}; (5.133) is \texttt{unifDetDom\_driftELK} and the
  assembly \texttt{sharpExpect\_step6\_driftSplit} discharges the hierarchy, the fast-decay claim
  and (5.133).  The good-set accounting uses \texttt{fastDecay\_integral\_of\_highProb}, which
  needs the event only with high probability, not at every $\omega$ -- Lemma 5.9 does not deliver
  the latter -- and \texttt{norm\_integral\_le\_add\_measure\_compl}, which does not require the
  good set to be measurable.

  \emph{Where the missing $A^{-1}$ comes from, and a hypothesis that cannot be derived.}  Each
  half gains its own $A^{-1}$ and for different reasons: on the $K$ half it is the cancellation in
  the expectation, (5.126) giving $\mathbb E\langle(G-m)E_a\rangle\prec A^{-2}$ where the pathwise
  bound is only $A^{-1}$; on the $L-K$ half it is pathwise, from taking the 3-loop to be $L-K$
  rather than $L$ ((2.78) gives $A^{-3}$, while (2.77) gives $L_3$ only $A^{-2}$) -- which is also
  why (5.77)'s third line is short one $A$ in \ref{thm:driftdef}.  Lemma 5.15 has to cover both
  charges here (\texttt{gloop\_one\_conj}, \texttt{lkPath\_one\_conj}); without that, (5.134)
  covers one of the four $\sigma\in\{+,-\}^2$.  The hypothesis \texttt{hG} of
  \texttt{sharpExpect\_step6} is \emph{not derivable as stated}: it offers only a uniform bound
  $\Lambda$, while $E^{(G)}=W\sum_{a,b}S^{(B)}_{ab}X_aY_b$ with $\sum_{a,b}S^{(B)}_{ab}=L$, so a
  uniform bound can only give $W L\Lambda$, whereas the paper's $W\ell_u$ comes from the
  \emph{decay} of the 3-loop, which \texttt{hG} does not supply (paper-deltas \#127).  Hence
  \texttt{driftBound\_driftEG} proves the conclusion (5.135) directly and
  \texttt{sharpExpect\_step6\_driftEG} has no \texttt{hG}; the mixed second moment
  $\mathbb E[(L-K)_1(L-K)_3]$ is not needed either, the $L-K$ half being pathwise.  What remains
  hypothetical is sample-side or envelope-side: the decay and power counting of \texttt{EGInputs}
  and \texttt{FDInputs} (same source as \texttt{DriftInputs}), the polynomial envelope and
  measurability conditions, and integrability of the one-loop at both charges.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}\leanok
  $\ell_u^2(1-u)$ is non-increasing; $\int_s^u\eta_v^{-1}dv\le2\log W$ by (2.72).
  The shape is pinned numerically by \texttt{scale\_shape\_check}
  ($W\ell A^{-4}=\eta^{-1}A^{-3}$) and \texttt{pathwise\_shape\_is\_one\_power\_weaker}, which
  records that the gain over the pathwise shape is one power of $A$ and not two: a mistyped
  exponent fails to compile.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[$n$-chain estimate, Lemma A.2]\label{lem:A.2}
  \lean{RBM.sum_Sblk_mul_eq, RBM.Sblk_symm, RBM.sum_Sblk_mul_le, RBM.sqrt_sum_Sblk_quad_le, RBM.norm_quad_H_le_of_LDE, RBM.norm_gchainMinor_sub_gchain_le_sum, RBM.norm_gchainMinor_sub_gchain_le_div, RBM.sq_norm_gchainMinor_le, RBM.LoopBound, RBM.ChainLDE16, RBM.ChainLDE19, RBM.chainBound, RBM.chainScaleUpTo, RBM.norm_quad_gchain_le, RBM.norm_gchain_off_le, RBM.bootstrap_A14_A17, RBM.normSq_gchain_double_le, RBM.XiDiag_XiOff_step, RBM.XiDiag_XiOff_le_chainBound, RBM.lemma_A2}
  \leanok\uses{lem:A, lem:4.1}
  Given $1/G_{ii}=O(1)$, the loop bound (2.77) and [39, Lemma 3.3] as hypotheses: the induction
  (A.7) with (A.14), (A.17) gives (A.3) $|(C_nE_cC_n^\dagger)_{ii}|\le C\Phi^{-2n+1}$ and (A.4)
  $|(C_n)_{ij}|\le C\Phi^{-n+1/2}$ for $\Phi=W\ell\eta$ large, with explicit constants.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}\leanok
  (A.12)(A.13) are replaced by the one-sided comparison $|C^{(ii)}-C|\le c(B+\Xi^{(o)}_n)\Phi^{-n}$;
  (A.16)'s error by positivity of $CEC^\dagger$; a real-number bootstrap closes (A.14)(A.17).
\end{proof}

RBM.Gauss.exists_gap_of_abs_lt_two, RBM.Gauss.exists_norm_Kval_le_upto, RBM.Gauss.hKb_flow, RBM.Gauss.eventually_le_rpow_mono, RBM.Gauss.eventually_etaT_inv_le_rpow, RBM.Gauss.flow_sharpLmK_of_hHol_flow, RBM.Gauss.exists_hHol_flow_inputs, RBM.Gauss.hnum_of_endpoints, RBM.Gauss.hnum_endpoint_of_three, RBM.Gauss.one_add_norm_mul_le, RBM.norm_Uker_fastDecay_le_sumZero, RBM.SumZeroDyn.norm_Uker_sumZero_scale_le, RBM.Gauss.one_add_norm_edge_eq, RBM.Gauss.gridS_window_ratio, RBM.Gauss.gridS_window_len, RBM.Gauss.no_const_gridS_ratio, RBM.Gauss.no_const_edge_row_on_gridS, RBM.Gauss.norm_xi2_eq_one, RBM.Gauss.no_const_hkerC_on_gridS, RBM.Gauss.no_const_hker2C_on_gridS, RBM.Gauss.momNorm_const, RBM.Gauss.momNorm_const_mul,
